#6971. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#6969. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

As an umbrella term, education technology encompasses everything from learning management systems and student information systems to productivity tools and AI platforms. More specific language clarifying the difference between ed tech and consumer-facing tools would allow state leaders to match the right evidence, expertise, and policy to the right questions.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#6964. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#6963. This Designer’s Long Island Family Home Has Been 40 Years in the Making

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat is a laboratory for color, collecting, and the occasional improbable antique.

Phillip Thomas’s first shop had a captive clientele. As a child, he commandeered the mudroom of his family’s house in Bellport, New York, filled it with yard-sale finds he had repainted or refinished, and ushered his parents’ friends inside. He called the enterprise Phillip & Co. (“There was no Co.,” he says. It was just him.) His customers, he recalls, were more or less obligated to buy something.

The Phillip & Co. space is now a pantry for extra tableware, but Thomas is still rearranging the house around it. His family has owned the late-19th-century home for 40 years, and the New York–based interior designer returns almost every weekend. The four-bedroom house includes two porches, a sunny sitting room overlooking the bay, and a living room anchored by a large fireplace. Thomas still sleeps in his childhood bedroom when there, and his parents, siblings, nieces, and friends gather at the property regularly, too.

Thomas’s Manhattan apartment is white and contemporary, filled with Latin American art. Bellport called for something different: “an American cottage by the sea,” he says. “I really just want to feel comfortable and I want everyone around me to feel at ease.” Getting there has involved decades of experimentation, a few unlikely purchases, and regular consultation with a formidable client committee: his family. “It’s a democracy,” he says. “It’s not a dictatorship.”

His love of Bellport extends well beyond the borders of his family’s property. The South Shore village has long been a mecca for creatives. The South Bay Art Association held its first official meeting at the village’s community center in 1957. Thomas, who has been coming to Bellport since he was four, remembers spending time in that same building as a child. In 2019, he led the renovation of its basement bowling alley and recreation space through the Bellport Village Program Fund.

There, he took Long Island’s Great South Bay as a starting point, introducing blue floors punctuated with red circles that evoked buoys, which village workers helped paint. The project restored a childhood gathering place for another generation, with color doing much of the work on a limited budget. It is a public expression of the instincts that have shaped his family’s house: keep what matters, reconsider what no longer works, and make the result inviting enough that people will use it.

At home, those decisions are rarely final. The dining room has gone from lacquered hunter green to red to its current painted pattern, where undulating lines surround a large, exuberantly colored painting. Elsewhere, Thomas used stencils to give walls the irregularity he seeks out in handmade things. “Having something painted onto the walls gives the room soul,” he says. “There is perfection in imperfection.”

The collections have accumulated on similar terms. Antique tools, gathered with his father over decades, line a hallway in carefully ordered arrangements. Thomas admires them as objects made by hand to facilitate other handmade things; displayed together, their worn wooden forms are sculptural. In the powder room, a coal-mining cart found a second career as a basin, paired with a vintage mirror frame fitted with glass cut to accommodate the plumbing. “You’d be surprised what I’ll put in the back of my car,” he says.

Other pieces traveled even farther. A campy painting of dogs playing poker came with his mother’s family from Yugoslavia to northern Chile, then to Santiago. After his grandmother died, the family agreed it belonged in Bellport, where it hangs in Thomas’s studio. The family’s routines shift with the seasons, which in turn shape how they use the house. Winter draws everyone toward the living-room fireplace, where the sofa is covered in a Schumacher fabric reminiscent of an American rag rug and throw pillows are made from textiles Thomas found in Turkey. From May through October, life moves out to the porch, where varied flexible seating facilitates long dinners and several conversations at once.

For the porch fireplace, Thomas collaborated with local artist Jay Sylvester on a sculpture of foraged branches surrounding a bull’s-eye mirror. The reflection ensures that even someone seated with their back to the bay gets a view.

Even the garden has benefited from his instinct to collect. Thomas acquired Chilean sculptures, including a Corten-steel piece inspired by wind-gnarled trees. For the latest overhaul, he collaborated with John Beitel of Landspace Inc., who has tended the gardens for 35 years. Together, they shaped a landscape where formal beds give way to looser planting along paths that frame views of the water, with red dogwood branches providing color through winter.

For all its evolution, the house’s greater purpose has remained remarkably consistent. Thomas’s nieces now invite their own friends; bedrooms are invariably full on summer weekends; and everyone helps cook or set the table. His mother, Sava, spends time on the porch before the household wakes, with a cup of coffee and the breeze coming off the water. After 40 years, she is still photographing the view. “I take photos and photos and they’re all the same,” she says. “But it’s beautiful.”

#6960. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#6958. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6955. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6947. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6940. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#6939. The 12 Best Amber Colognes to Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are the season’s top cold-weather compliment getters.

It has been an unusually warm fall so far, but today, at least in New York City, there’s finally a chill in the air. I got so excited when I saw the temperature was in the low 50s this morning that I may have rushed the season a tad and put on a thick sweater under an insulated jacket. So, I was sweating in the subway, but at least I smelled good—because when it comes to cold-weather fragrance, I know what I’m doing.

If you’re looking to add to your winter cologne rotation, there’s no better place to start than a good amber scent. When you think of fragrance as an accessory, it becomes something you strive to match with your outfit, and amber colognes pair perfectly with corduroy, thick coats, and soft scarves. But what exactly does amber smell like? After all, the kind of amber that probably comes to mind—fossilized tree amber—doesn’t actually have much of a scent.

Well, when we say “amber” in the fragrance world, it really refers to a blend of natural resins and synthetics that combine to produce a warm, rich, and sweet smell. Some classic scent notes that make up the amber fragrance family include labdanum (a resinous extract from a Mediterranean shrub), benzoin (a sweet, balsamic tree resin), ambroxan (a synthetic fragrance molecule with a warm, woody aroma), vanilla, and tonka bean.

Here, we’ve pulled together our definitive list of the top amber scents for men, covering every vibe, lifestyle, and scent story you’re trying to tell. Whether you’re leaning into smoky sophistication, something on the sweeter side, or going for “I naturally smell this good,” there’s something here for everyone. And with the holidays fast approaching, consider these foolproof gift options for anyone on your list who appreciates smelling like they have their life together.

Inspired by the luxury fashion label’s most iconic men’s silhouette, this fragrance is designed to capture the essence of a tuxedo, using notes that convey sophistication and sharp black-tie tailoring into scent. Grounded in earthy patchouli and layered with subtle spice, it radiates a refined, confident energy that’s perfect for winter yet versatile enough for wearing it year-round.

The purpose of Maison Margiela’s Replica line is that the scents are made to capture memories and moods, the scent of a specific moment. By the Fireplace is easily one of the most evocative scents in the collection—my personal favorite, for what it’s worth—because smoke and warm chestnut instantly transport you somewhere near a warm crackling fire.

It starts with a bit of spice and smoke, and once it settles on the skin, that initial smokiness softens into a smooth, vanilla warmth. The rest of the fragrance leans very heavily into the sweetness of amber. It’s a compliment-getter, if that’s what you’re after.

If you want to channel your inner Alexander Skarsgård in Pillion (IYKYK) with a scent that radiates rugged confidence, subtle earthiness, and that intoxicating new-leather smell that only comes from a new pair of boots or a leather trench, look no further. Malin + Goetz’s Leather Eau de Parfum has been a staple in my collection for years

It’s leathery and musky, but it has a good hit of cedar that combines to leans it into the world of amber. It also has a lot of floral notes as it dries on the skin to balance it all out. It’s a nicely layered fragrance. A purist might not call this amber, but we think it’s well within the sweet, resinous world of these other fragrances.

Not ready to commit to the full bottle? The unisex cologne also comes as a perfume oil in a compact rollerball perfect for a trial run or as a stocking stuffer.

As I noted in my intro, amber fragrances can take on a variety of scent profiles, all capturing the warmth and depth of true amber. Rich gourmand options like Horace’s Vintage Vanilla are a great pick if you love scents inspired by the culinary world, but don’t worry, it’s expertly balanced with a mix of woodiness and spice so you won’t end up smelling like you went overboard on your marshmallow-y Victoria’s Secret body mist.

Don’t even want to smell like an edible vanilla confection? No problem. The brand’s signature Oud Rose is another solid amber-forward scent that leans on floral notes to balance its intensity.

Diptyque is another one of those bougie fragrance houses everyone recognizes, yet for some reason I rarely reach for their scents over my tried-and-true favorites. But after smelling L’Eau Trois Eau de Toilette, I might have to rethink that stance. This one immediately transported me to being in Italy and stepping into a Duomo, where incense and myrrh linger in the air.

It takes a surprisingly verdant and green approach to the amber family, but those resinous top notes anchor everything with a spicy-smoky warmth. The result is a scent that feels refined, atmospheric, and just mysterious enough to keep you coming back for another sniff.

Sure, amber scents can be sweet and gourmand, but they can also be spicy and sophisticated. Giardini di Toscana’s Bianco Oro is a great example of the latter. At first spritz, you mostly get spicy pink and black pepper with a backdrop of amber, but as the scent lingers, the rich amber notes take center stage along with smooth musks and creamy woods. As it fades, it becomes a bit powdery, but the trio of spices, amber, and musks are still there. It’s the perfect scent to wear out to a nice dinner or evening event, but it works just as well in the daytime.

It’s a general rule that amber and woods go great together, since amber provides a warm richness that blends well with dry, crisp woods. That combination is at the foundation of Dolce & Gabbana’s The One for Men EDP, but before we get there, we actually get a touch of bright and juicy citrus from Tarocco orange and grapefruit as well as a natural, herbal hint of clary sage at the heart that adds a bit of earthiness to this fragrance. Amber and woods mingle at the base, grounding the lighter, more vibrant opening in something warm and rich.

Guerlain makes a handful of luxury amber fragrances, but Tobacco Honey is the one that stands out from the rest. It’s an intense blend of—you guessed it—tobacco leaves and golden honey, which creates a beautifully bold contrast of smoky and sweet. It emphasizes the sweet side of the ambery tobacco accord at the heart of this scent, enveloping the dry tobacco notes in vanilla, tonka bean, and sesame to create a rich, creamy heart that gets lifted up by vibrant, sweet honey. If you’ve ever wanted to try a tobacco fragrance but worried it would smell too smoky (or too much like cigarettes), this is the one that will change your mind.

For something a bit lighter and more airy than the mostly rich and deep amber scents on this list, try Golden Morning from Lafco’s new (and first-ever) personal fragrance collection. Like a cup of hot chamomile tea with honey, it’s soothing, warm, and a little sweet. Bright and citrusy at the top with bergamot and apple blossom, it then introduces chamomile and a floral heart of lavender, violet leaf, and rose. The dry down is smooth and sweet with honey, tonka bean, amber, and musks.

Okay, I’m not saying the other fragrances on this list can’t also be considered “bougie,” but Maison Francis Kurkdjian Paris’ Baccarat Rouge 540 takes luxury to an entirely new level. And yes, while the price tag is particularly eye-watering, I have to admit: this scent is absolutely worth it. A perfectly balanced blend of woody, floral, and ambery notes, this fragrance uses high-quality ingredients with a higher molecular weight, meaning they cling to the skin longer and create a richer scent profile with impressive staying power. In short, a little goes a very long way with this one…if that helps you justify the price tag.

Sitting somewhere between gourmand and citrus on the fragrance spectrum, Arquiste’s L’Or de Louis is one of those amber scents that just radiates richness. The 24K gold flakes are a little too on-the-nose, but the the story behind the fragrance does a better job of telling a story of opulence. It was inspired by the moments when King Louis XIV and his court strolled Versailles’ Grand Orangery, enjoying the flowering orange trees warmed by bonfires.

It also won The Fragrance Foundation’s Indie Fragrance of the Year Award in 2024, so the hoity-toity storytelling is backed by a scent that actually smells…well…good. The result is a golden aura of perfectly blended smokiness, fruitiness, and depth. A fragrance truly fit for a king.

If your nose is on the more sensitive side and you’re looking for a warm, enveloping scent that doesn’t overwhelm with heavy muskiness, D’Orsay’s Te dire oui. V.H. Eau de Parfum is a perfect choice. Built on leather, it avoids the overpowering muskiness found in many amber and leather fragrances, opting instead for a cleaner, sportier interpretation rounded out by a subtle saffron and rose finish.

This aligns perfectly with the brand’s philosophy, which favors fragrances that act as personal rituals—subtle companions that are seamlessly woven into everyday life—rather than scents that dominate every room you enter.

#6935. House Republicans Couldn’t Have Picked a Worse Minority Speaker

So much for cleaning house. Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio has been a loyal foot soldier of the MAGA regime for years now.

Politico has a very strange story about the battle for Republican leadership in the House of Representatives. Speaker Mike Johnson already has scratched himself from the race to become the next minority leader. And here is Politico’s take, which seems very, very odd.

So far, this is a run-of-the-mill state of the losers’ postelection story. The losing side, particularly a catastrophically losing side, always spends some time in public introspection. It’s almost a kind of penance. But here’s where the story spins out.

As I’m sure Stutzman knows, on any issue that both involves the administration and drove voters in the midterm elections, there’s not a dime’s worth of difference between Scalise and Jordan (R-Van Heusen). They have both been loyal foot soldiers of the MAGA regime. Both of them supported at least one objection to certify the results in 2020. On Tuesday, Jordan thumped Scalise and became the GOP leader, 106–73, and it will be interpreted as another defeat for Johnson’s old leadership team. But the promotion of Jordan doesn’t answer any of the problems that contributed to the loss of Congress by the Republicans. Jordan’s knees haven’t stopped jerking since 2017. He hunted every snipe at which the administration pointed, even the ones that didn’t exist, which was a substantial number of them. He was rude and approximately as committed to bipartisanship as a manhole cover. If the primary issue in the election was corruption, Jordan has made a career out of supplying alibis.

I hope Speaker-in-Waiting Jeffries realizes all of this. This is the legislative equivalent of Stephen Miller running the White House. I’d say the Republicans could have found someone more dedicated to getting things done, but there isn’t anybody like that left.

#6927. Actually, Robert Pattinson Would Make a Perfect James Bond

My colleagues and editors are sick of hearing me say it, but I believe it with my whole chest: The actor playing James Bond should never be more famous than James Bond. There’s something about Bond—his cultural footprint and the dangerous yet seductive life he leads in his stories—that makes him too big to be a footnote in a great actor’s résumé. James Bond creates leading men; James Bond is not for leading men to create.

So you have to know that it’s not easy for me to say this: Robert Pattinson would be fantastic and exactly the new James Bond we’re looking for.

You’ve probably heard by now that Amazon, which acquired full creative control over all things Bond from longtime owners Eon Productions and the Broccoli family, has been searching for the next 007 in a movie to be directed by Denis Villeneuve.

What was once an arena of speculation has exploded into a circus, with different names being bandied about at once—namely Callum Turner, Josh O’Connor, and Jack Lowden. (There is obviously a specific type of white man they’re going for here.) Just when it seemed as if the new Bond would finally be unveiled, there came a rush of two really big names suddenly in the running. Among them is Pattinson, reported by The Hollywood Reporter on November 6, along with Nicholas Hoult. Pattinson secretly auditioned for the role during a break in filming for The Batman: Part II, which halted filming in October when Matt Reeves attended to a personal matter.

No shade to Hoult or any of the other actors, but Pattinson is now the most interesting candidate despite a whiff of overexposure this year. Between The Drama in the spring, The Odyssey in the summer, Primetime in the fall, and soon Dune: Part Three in the dead of winter, 2026 has been shaping up into a banner year for Pattinson. Not since Twilight has the man been so prolific on our screens.

But even before the year began, Pattinson had long ago outgrown his image of a tween heartthrob. He’s a prodigious talent capable of daring choices and executing on them in a way you can’t ever expect. (Who else would say cock like this?) His popular line from The Odyssey—“Somebody get these beggars out of here!”—didn’t become a meme on its own. Pattinson made beggars sound like a slur, and in the same breath, his nervous blinking and quivering smile cemented Antinous as an unconfident weasel whose puffed-out chest was, in fact, filled with hot air. Unlike previous Batman actors, who often played the Caped Crusader like a knight in matte black armor, Pattinson’s interpretation was/is that of an antisocial weirdo everyone stares at like a freak. Which he is.

Like Batman, Bond has also been a role that people misunderstand, sometimes even by the men who play him. (It is not my biases talking here; I really do think Daniel Craig is the only actor to ever nail the nuances and pathology of the character.) Bond is stylish and can say the right thing at the right time, be it to enemies or lovers. But he’s not a dashing or noble hero. He’s a brute in Brunello Cucinelli. A thug with a $9,000 Omega. He literally kills for a living—and had to kill to get that right—and his attachment to his job is downright destabilizing.

Recall in Skyfall, after Judi Dench’s M orders Moneypenny to shoot, Bond copes with endless drinking, womanizing, and gambling. That might sound like a fun weekend in Vegas, but in the context of Bond, his fallout from discovering his expendability led him to a really, really dark path. It is not normal to drink shots with a scorpion on your hand, nor is it healthy to be up in the morning reaching for booze from behind the bar. Bond might live a life many of us men dream of, but it comes at a severe cost. I don’t know what kind of Bond movie Villeneuve has in mind, but if the creative team is lining up like this, we’re in for something special.

You can’t exactly guesstimate what kind of 007 Pattinson might be based on his previous roles. There is truly little common ground between Batman and Bond. But one thing is for sure: If Pattinson gets the part, we are in for a goddamn show.

#6921. So Is Lanterns Canceled Or What?

It was one of the best new HBO shows in recent memory, but it seems like that won’t matter at all.

You know what happens when you blow up a universe? You’re left sifting through the rubble. Since the news that James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran have been ousted as co-CEOs and cochairmen of DC Studios, there are many questions about the future of their DC Universe. Chief among them is Lanterns. So is Lanterns canceled? That’s what everyone wants to know.

The confusion started when Variety published its report about the exit of Gunn and Safran on November 10. The brunt of the story details how Gunn and Safran’s leadership of the studio was a modest, but not big enough, success for Warner Bros. (and now Skydance) brass. “Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best—with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags,” wrote Variety. “Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big—and lost too much—on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.”

In the story, Variety explored the studio’s TV efforts, which we should note have plenty of asterisks. Under Gunn and Safran’s leadership, DC Studios released The Penguin, a spin-off of The Batman that won awards; the caveat is that it was already in development before Gunn and Safran took over. There was also the second season of Peacemaker, a series created by Gunn (as a spin-off of his 2021 movie The Suicide Squad) but whose creation also, weirdly, predates Gunn’s reign over DC. It was also the second season of a television show that premiered three years earlier.

This leads to Lanterns, the first DC show to grow from conception to air under Gunn and Safran. In Variety’s original version of its story, the outlet’s phrasing about the fate of Lanterns made the Internet jump, as it suggested the show, which cultivated a dedicated audience late in the summer, was canceled. “An insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season,” Variety wrote.

Hours later, Variety changed its story to its current phrasing (the new words in bold): “Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show has yet to be renewed for a second season.”

Variety also added the same source, saying, “People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show.”

You can see why there’s confusion. Gunn and Safran’s dismissal from DC was sudden, blindsiding even the men themselves (according to trades like The Hollywood Reporter) at a time when DC Studios seemed to be in a steady rhythm. Sure, this year’s Supergirl was a fumble, but last year’s Superman had gotten the refreshed DCU off on the right foot, and the recent mid-budget horror thriller Clayface grossed a clean $100 million. Though none of the Gunn/Safran–era movies were eye-watering successes, you also can’t measure long-term impact and sustained popularity of a franchise in box-office milestones alone. That’s not how art works.

It’s especially confusing with Lanterns. Back in October, outlets like Variety reported strong viewership figures for the series on HBO and HBO Max. In Skydance’s own third-quarter earnings call, the studio touted Lanterns as a rousing success, with an average of 22 million global viewers, “among HBO Max’s strongest launches” ever for the platform. To now read from insider sources claiming Lanterns wasn’t “quite capturing the zeitgeist” is downright absurd and frankly an insult to anyone’s intelligence.

Is Lanterns canceled? The good news is it isn’t. The bad news is that it isn’t canceled yet. Lanterns was a key part of Gunn’s grand plans for the DCU. Now that he’s been removed—and his future at the company unclear, despite more projects in development under his guidance—the fate of Lanterns is uncertain, as is the whole DCU.

A likely scenario is that Skydance will burn through all the remaining DC projects and dump them on HBO Max—stuff like Gunn’s Superman sequel Man of Tomorrow, the mockumentary comedy The People vs. Gorilla Grodd, and the animated film Dynamic Duo, which is said to be a highly experimental project using puppetry and animation. Lanterns season 2 could be among them, perhaps fast-tracked to HBO Max, where it would get a swift cancellation regardless of its performance (especially now that audiences will know not to get invested), or it simply doesn’t get made at all. But it’s anyone’s guess what will actually happen.

Whether audiences will get to see John Stewart be the Green Lantern is now in the hands of new studio leaders like Jeremy Latcham, who will replace Gunn and Safran starting December 1. We’ll see if Latcham leads with willpower or through fear.

#6920. The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both hit theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop—namely Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi films of the past five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then, these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and eccentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly terrifying story follows a medical courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptations over the past few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box-office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

#6918. Wondering Why Taylor Swift Doesn’t Perform at Festivals Like Coachella? We Did Some Theorizing and Digging

Coachella just released its 2027 lineup, and for those convinced Taylor Swift would be headlining...sorry! While the lineup is absolutely stacked (Madonna! SZA! Dua Lipa!), Taylor is not one of the featured artists. Which...isn’t that surprising. After all, she doesn’t historically do festivals (she is kinda busy casually breaking every touring record and selling out stadiums).

While Taylor hasn’t explained why she’s never headlined Coachella (or other major festival like Glastonbury), it doesn’t seem like a stretch to assume that crowd control could be an issue for her. Taylor is used to performing in the biggest possible stadiums across the world, but they have infrastructure in place to handle crowds even bigger than Coachella, not to mention built-in security. Coachella, as organized as it is, has a somewhat more chaotic vibe, and crowd control is definitely less of a thing.

But if you’re looking for a chatty source who’s commented on the subject, someone told The Huffington Post allllllll the way back in 2014 that “Taylor doesn't like festivals. So for any bookies reading... I think Taylor likes doing her own show because she's in control and it's how she wants it to be. With Glastonbury or any festival, you're at the mercy of the people who run the festival. She wants it all to be perfect.”

Note: Taylor might not like performing at festivals (or at least didn’t in 2014), but she’s definitely a fan in general—she at Travis were spotted at Coachella in 2024 to support her close friend and collaborator Jack Antonoff.

Meanwhile, Taylor did open up about why she’s never performed at the Superbowl halftime show (which fans have also been wondering about), explaining that she’s too locked in on Travis. “That is violent chess,” Tay said of watching football. “That is gladiators without swords. That is dangerous. The whole season I am locked in on what that man is doing on the field. Can you imagine if he's out there every single week putting his life on the line, doing this very dangerous, very high-pressure, high-intensity sport, and I'm like, ‘I wonder what my choreo should be. I think we should do two verses of ‘Shake it Off' into ‘Blank Space’ into ‘Cruel Summer’ would be great.’”

She added that the decision “is nothing to do with Travis, he would love for me to do it. I'm just too locked in.”

Point is: Taylor will likely be sticking to stadium tours, and we’re truly blessed.

#6916. Kings of Leon’s Caleb Followill Is Finally Ready to Bare It All

The lead singer tells Esquire in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned” that the band’s new record features its most honest music to date.

Last year, Kings of Leon was forced to cancel part of its summer tour. The band’s lead singer, Caleb Followill, broke his foot and severely displaced his heel during a freak accident that he jokes was “a rock ’n’ roll injury.” But really, he admits to Esquire, he was just playing tag with his kids—a little roughhousing that turned into eight months of recovery before he could walk back onto the stage.

“I was kind of feeling sorry for myself,” Followill tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The singer felt like he had let the band’s fans down. He turned 44, he lost his mother not too long ago, and he channeled all that grief and negative energy into a new, stripped-down Kings of Leon record, O My Beloved, which was released on November 6. “I had a weight on my shoulders,” he says. “And one day I picked up the guitar, and it was almost like I walked through a door and I could see things a little more clearly.”

O My Beloved is the most melancholic and reflective album in Kings of Leon’s ten-album discography. Followill tells Esquire that he wanted to speak about his childhood and the family that shaped him on the new songs because he used to feel embarrassed about his past. His family grew up poor—traveling around the South and living out of their car at times. (Kings of Leon is a family band, with Caleb joined by his brothers Nathan and Jared Followill, as well as their cousin Matthew Followill.) But now Caleb wants to pass all his memories on to his kids, and he doesn’t want them to feel ashamed about any part of the journey that led them here.

“When I was writing these songs, my mind just kept going back to small moments in my life—moments that had no significance,” he says. “I wanted to tell these stories, and I wanted to be honest about parts of my life that I didn’t want to be honest about before."

O My Beloved is the band’s first self-produced record since parting ways with RCA, its longtime record label. It’s also the band’s tenth studio album, celebrating nearly 20 years since the 2008 record Only by the Night rocketed it to stardom thanks to hit songs “Sex on Fire” and “Use Somebody.”

“I was always scared of fame, and I think I still am,” Followill says. “That’s the good thing about having your brothers next to you. They’ll rip you right back down, shine a light in your eyes, and say, ‘Hey man, this can all come crumbling down and we’ll be back in our crumbling house.’ ”

You can listen to the episode on YouTube and Spotify. Elsewhere, Followill shares his secret to 15 years of marriage (to model Lily Aldridge) and what he’s learned from touring with Pearl Jam, U2, and Zach Bryan.

#6912. Here’s Your Complete Release Schedule for The Rings of Power Season 3

Because we know your time is ... precious.

One does not simply walk into Mordor, but it is rather easy to binge a new season. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power is back on Prime Video for season 3 to continue the Second Age of Middle-earth. The Rings have been forged, yet there is still one more ring to rule them all. But how can anyone from the realms of men keep up with Rings of Power?

In The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power season 3, Sauron (Charlie Vickers) has finally dropped his facade. No longer hiding behind fake names like Halbrand or Annatar, he is truly himself: Sauron, the Dark Lord. And the Dark Lord now races to forge the fabled One Ring from his seat in Barad-dûr.

Meanwhile, as his orc armies eclipse Middle-earth, his war rages with the Elves—concentrated in Lindon and Rivendell—who begin an alliance with Dwarves and men. And a few wizards thrown in for good measure.

Four years since it premiered on Prime Video, The Rings of Power is officially at the halfway mark of its predetermined five-season run. While it’s still hard for the show to escape the shadow of Peter Jackson’s immortal trilogy, Rings of Power is still a handsome effort at probing all that went down before a Hobbit picked up a ring in a cave.

You don’t need Palantir to know when The Rings of Power drops new episodes on Prime Video. Here’s how and when you can watch new episodes of the Lord of the Rings series.

The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power season 3 premiered its first four episodes on November 11, exclusively on Prime Video. They are available to watch right now. The rest of the season will roll out weekly in batches, with two episodes per week until November 25. Which is awesome! Once you’ve seen every episode, you can roll right into your annual post-Thanksgiving trilogy rewatch.

As a quick note: With Prime Video originals, there’s a favorable chance you can watch new episodes a few hours earlier. Most of the platform’s shows actually drop new episodes the night before. That’s what happened with The Rings of Power season 3, as the first few episodes were made available to stream on November 10 at 8:00 p.m. ET even though Prime Video promoted November 11 as the official date. So if you’re really impatient, check for new episodes the night before.
