#6873. 5 Fall Trends Designers Think Will Still Look Good Next Spring

Above: A hallway view of Eric Egan’s Milan apartment.

If any season has the power to inspire a complete aesthetic overhaul, it’s fall. Nature’s changing palette inspires us to follow suit, as each year our mood boards make new space for jewel tones, cozy textures, and a bottomless candle budget. Autumnal trends are so alluring, it almost feels like a risky time to make long-term design decisions. But with the right consideration, that seasonal inspiration can translate into choices that look good year-round.

This fall in particular, designers are urging clients to trust their guts—making sure they see themselves in the spaces they create, rather than blindly following the latest fads. “For me, a trend has to be authentic or it’s just noise,” says designer Chad Dorsey, who’s eager to ditch the spare minimalism of years past for looks with more personality. ELLE Decor A-Lister Kerry Joyce seconds the motion: “By prioritizing personalized storytelling, a space becomes evergreen, evolving as smaller objects are swapped out over time.”

But an expert nudge in the right direction never hurts. This fall, top designers are gravitating toward creative color-drenching, cleverly concealed storage, tactile upholstery, and rooms built around art. Read on for their takes on the fall 2026 trends worth investing in, plus tips on building (as Dorsey puts it) “spaces designed to be lived in, not looked at.”

Fabric and upholstery are trending ultra plush and textured for fall, but when thoughtfully integrated, they feel more timeless than seasonal. “Design is shifting toward how a space feels, not just how it looks,” says Joyce, who’s seeing an increased popularity in fabrics like mohair, velvet, cashmere, and alpaca hair. “This focus on texture does more than elevate a room's aesthetic,” he explains. “It creates a sensory experience that comforts the soul.”

Start your bedroom redesign this fall and your space will strike the perfect balance between fresh and lived-in by spring. Between client requests and a personal home build, Amber Lewis has bedrooms on the brain right now. One trend takes the lead in her eyes: “I love matching the walls to the bed upholstery and bedding so the whole room feels wrapped in one warm color family. It’s calm and peaceful, and that enveloping feeling works in any season.”

Luckily, this cozy trend doesn’t require a complete room overhaul: “The easiest place to start is the bed itself. Reupholster your headboard in a pattern you love, or drape a vintage suzani across the foot of the bed,” Lewis says. “One move like that changes the whole mood of the room without starting over.” To bring in a little dimension and pop, she recommends adding a singular statement, like a lone striped pillow.

If your primary suite needs a more holistic update, Lewis recommends creating a small sitting area. Sourcing vintage chairs and small sofas for primary suites this fall has given Lewis a new perspective on where bedroom design is headed: “True sanctuaries that feel layered, personal, and built for actually living in.”

Color drenching isn’t going anywhere for fall 2026—or long after, according to Dorsey. When choosing a shade for a room, he finds that any color works harder when it’s layered and intentional. Color capping—a term coined by Benjamin Moore referring to painting a room in multiple tones from one color family—can help keep a space from feeling flat or overwhelmed beneath a statement hue.

According to Dorsey, the staying power of color drenching goes hand in hand with the shift away from spareness and toward “collected” spaces: “Neither is really a ‘look;’ they’re about living with things you love, which is why they’ll carry well into spring.”

Designer Regan Baker says that cleverly hidden space solutions are also on the rise. “It could be a TV that slides behind a commissioned art panel, a Murphy bed tucked into a wall of millwork or that serves as a desk when it's stored, or a peg wall that’s a place to hang a jacket one minute and a standing desk the next,” she says. These solutions are tailored to her clients’ spaces (rather than tied to a season or palette), which gives them staying power.

This concept is an easy way to turn the corner you’ve never known how to style into your favorite part of the room. “In an existing home, look at the awkward spots, like an alcove or the wall around a fireplace, and treat built-ins there as a design moment instead of an afterthought,” Baker advises.

If there’s one built-to-last fall trend most designers can agree on, it’s added eccentricity. “Homes are steering away from the sterile, overnight look of a showroom delivery,” Joyce says. “The defining philosophy now focuses on layering modern staples with antiques, secondhand finds, travel mementos, and new pieces that carry a sense of patina.”

Dorsey describes this style as “collected rather than prescribed,” where a high-low mix of vintage and custom elements gel together. And while it may feel loftier than a trending, sold-out piece, Baker says that most clients start with an artistic anchor, then build around it: “We’re designing more homes around a piece of art than around a color trend. That might be wallpaper by local designers, a commissioned piece, or even a palette pulled from a lichen-covered rock found on the property.”

One key takeaway? Curate like a collector. Your reward may be a space that never goes out of style.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6865. ‘DWTS’ Fans Are Side-Eyeing Tyler Cameron After He Reposted Jokes About Harry Shum Jr.’s Emotional Salsa Routine

Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson’s salsa routine unexpectedly brought tears to the Dancing With the Stars ballroom (and it had nothing to do with Jenna’s broken nose).

Before he and Jenna hit their steps to Lady Gaga and Bruno Mars’s Grammy-winning ballad, “Die With a Smile,” the Glee alum opened up about his personal connection to the dance genre—and why he suddenly felt a wave of emotion during rehearsals.

“I was born in Costa Rica, so like people were dancing salsa, merengue, bachata, but I was always an observer. So I’m really excited to tap into my roots,” Harry said of his heritage during the intro package that played before his performance.

After he and Jenna went through their routine in the rehearsal studio, he started tearing up.

“I don’t think I’ve ever felt such a strong emotion damn after dancing. I didn’t realize it, but Salsa is very meaningful to me,” Harry admitted. “I’ve kind of avoided doing salsa dancing for so long. I associated it with the struggles that my family went through, and I went through, moving from Costa Rica to America. But I just got a wave of emotion of just being really sad that I’ve missed the joy of salsa dancing for so long.”

While DWTS fans generally empathized with Harry’s feelings surrounding the routine, others joked about the lack of context in how his package was edited and claimed his reaction was “performative.”

“There’s something very performative about him,” one person wrote, to which another responded, “No he’s very open and honest and kind. I love him.”

A viewer on Reddit separately came to Harry’s defense, writing, “Its always so sad to see someone feeling disconnected from their culture AND it should be cool for people to possibly connect with that and anyone hating has never experienced that emotion most likely.”

Another person who quietly weighed in on the rehearsal package? That’d be Bachelor Nation’s Tyler Cameron, who was eliminated from the competition on October 27.

Fans raised eyebrows when Tyler reposted a TikTok poking fun at Harry’s tearful experience. “Harry Shum jr when the chipotle worker asks if he wants salsa on his burrito bowl,” the post reads, over a video of American Horror Story star Jessica Lange crying.

Once DWTS devotees caught wind of Tyler’s repost, they couldn’t tell if the joke went too far.

“So strange considering Tyler’s still in the comments on literally everyone’s posts every week cheering them on :(,” one person wrote, while another called out the eliminated dancer, writing, “So distasteful for Tyler to repost.”

Another person shared, “i was liking tyler but he still is talking about getting voted off weeks later😭 he’s being a weirdo right now.”

As of right now, the video is still filed under Tyler’s TikTok reposts. He has yet to speak on it, if at all, but he did share his thoughts on Jenna Dewan and Tatyana Ali’s performances on his Instagram Stories posted on November 11.

Harry also hasn’t publicly responded to the online chatter surrounding his performance, but I’ll be keeping an eye out to see if anything else unfolds!

#6864. Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6858. Lauren Sánchez Bezos Matches Her Inky Red Bottoms With a Vintage Two-Tone Chanel Skirt

Lauren Sánchez Bezos has gone from television news anchor to talk show host to Bezos bride, and now, she’s making her name in the world of picture books. With the release of her debut children’s story, The Fly Who Flew to Space, back in 2024, Sánchez Bezos became a New York Times bestseller. And today, she released a new holiday-themed sequel and third installment in the series, titled The Fly Who Flew to the North Pole, which she promoted this morning with an appearance in New York City.

It may not be the holidays quite yet, but Sánchez Bezos showed off her unique, femme-fatale approach to yuletide style as she stopped by CBS Mornings to celebrate her book’s release. There, many of her dressing hallmarks were on full display. Luxury vintage? Check. A cinched silhouette? Check. Pops of bloody red? Check and check.

For the talk-show visit, Sánchez Bezos looked like a gothic Mrs. Claus in a black, skintight turtleneck from Wolford and a fuzzy black miniskirt that was cinched along the waist with a red tweed band that simulated a cummerbund.

The two-tone mini also acted as the perfect complement to the former journalist’s inky patent-leather heels from Christian Louboutin, which featured the brand’s signature red bottoms.

To complete her look, Mrs. Bezos popped on a pair of oversize aviator sunglasses and silver, bow-shaped earrings. Plus, she carried a clasp clutch printed with the cover of her newly-released book (a cheeky marketing tactic that she’s employed in the past).

No matter the occasion, Sánchez Bezos will find a way to let her personality shine through.

#6857. Julia Child’s Purple-Hued Cambridge Home Sells Just Days After Listing

The legendary TV chef’s Massachusetts house fetched $6.525 million—above the asking price.

In today’s real estate market, price cuts are increasingly common. But Julia Child’s former Cambridge home just bucked the trend, selling mere days after listing—and fetching above the asking price. The legendary TV chef’s longtime Massachusetts residence hit the market on September 23 for $6.475 million. By October 2, it had found a buyer. The sale officially closed on October 29, per Zillow. In the end, the six-bedroom Cape Cod–style house traded hands for $6.525 million, $50,000 above asking.

Child and her husband, Paul, lived in the house for decades, starting in 1961, after Paul retired from his job in the federal government. The estate is also where Child shot several popular TV shows, among them In Julia’s Kitchen with Master Chefs and Julia and Jacques Cooking at Home.

Painted a muted shade of purple, the 6,300-square-foot home sits on a quarter-acre lot just a few blocks from Harvard Yard. Dating to 1889, the residence at one time featured a custom kitchen designed especially for Child, with pegboard walls, a six-burner industrial Garland range, and maple countertops a few inches taller than average to suit her 6’3” frame.

In the wake of Paul’s 1994 death, Child continued to live in the residence until 2001, when she donated the property to Smith College, her alma mater. (The Massachusetts institution sold the home in 2002 for $2.35 million, per Zillow.) The famous kitchen, meanwhile, was meticulously deconstructed—down to its linoleum flooring—and rebuilt in D.C.’s National Museum of American History, where it remains on display today.

Since then, the new owners have revamped the three-story home’s kitchen, which now sports marble countertops, a metallic backsplash, Poggenpohl cabinetry, and a Sub-Zero refrigerator. The rest of the residence is equipped with light-filled interiors, hardwood floors, and eco-friendly additions like solar panels.

This summer, the Cambridge City Council passed a proposal to install a historical marker on the estate’s grounds, according to Boston.com.

By chance, another Child-linked property recently traded hands as well, albeit for a discount. In August, a Santa Barbara ranch where she filmed the 12-episode PBS series Dinner at Julia’s listed for just under $22 million. It sold on October 16 for $19 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6856. The “Choppy Rachel Bob” Is the Most Flattering Winter Hair Trend—How to Recreate

Jennifer Aniston, the inspiration that you are.

I may be dressing like a walking blanket thanks to the drop in temperature (comfort > style, am I right?), but at least my hair—and more specifically, my recently cut bob —looks great. There's something about winter that calls for a total beauty refresh (and in my case, a drop in fashion presentation, but we shall overlook that), and seemingly, Meghann Fahy would agree, having just debuted a chin-length style inspired by the ’90s. So long, Goldilocks!

The The White Lotus actor debuted the haircut in question on The TODAY Show earlier this week, where she spoke all about her role in new thriller The Good Daughter. And while we're, of course, excited to hear all about the series, it was the bob chat that piqued our interest.

When asked about her new ’do, Meghann shared that the reference photo she showed her hairstylist was of “the Rachel,” Jennifer Aniston's iconic haircut from Friends. And yes, that is thee haircut that sent approximately every woman with a television to her local hair salon in the mid-’90s.

"It's like looking in the mirror," Meghann joked when shown a picture of Rachel Green.

Now, while we're not talking an exact replica of the original, Meghann's take feels like a much more wearable update (because we can’t all have that ’90s volume at all times). So, if you, too, want to recreate the style for winter 2026, because why wouldn't you, ask for a blunt yet choppy silhouette to create the illusion of fuller strands, and a little textured wave for movement and body. Oh, and, of course, show a reference picture of Rachel. Duh!

And honestly, winter is the perfect time to try the shorter style. The neck-skimming length looks oh-so-chic when peeking out from oversized scarves, while the shape gives hair a little more structure when the weather is doing its absolute worst.

Rachel Green (and Meghann Fahy), you've done it again.

#6854. Artist Quentin Jones Launches a Surrealist Jewelry Collection

With Nocturne, the House of Quentin Jones founder brings the playful, sculptural language of her homewares to a collection of wearable art.

Above: Artist Quentin Jones wears pieces from her new jewelry collection while posing in front of tapestries Circe’s Garden and Western Moon.

British mixed-media artist Quentin Jones has quite the varied resume—one that spans artistic disciplines and collaborators as varied as Chanel, Apple, and Miley Cyrus. Now based in Brooklyn, her practice blends filmmaking, photography, collage, and painting.

Last year, Jones turned her eye towards interiors, launching a homeware line and design studio called House of Quentin Jones. The brand debuted with a collection of tapestries and ceramic sculptures that are both architectural and playful, leaning into Jones’s moody, dreamy look. Now, after a year of HOQJ, Jones is releasing new styles—and adding jewelry to the mix.

Nocturne, a collection of sculptural jewelry, translates Jones’s eclectic design language into wearable art. She pulled motifs like eyes, hands, and celestial forms from her tapestries and ceramics and incorporated them into the pieces. The collection features earrings, pendants, chains, and cuffs that balance black and white, pearl and onyx, organic and sculptural shapes.

“I’m interested in jewelry as sculpture,” Jones tells ELLE Decor. “A cuff wraps around an arm, a collar frames the neck, a tassel moves. The body becomes part of the composition. I want the pieces to have a presence of their own, while leaving room for the personality of the person wearing them.”

As she enters this new category, Jones is also beefing up her existing offerings. She’s adding new quilted tapestries to the studio’s repertoire and iterating on previous designs. With these new pieces, she says, “The palette has become softer and earthier—ivory, sand, and burgundy—but the compositions still have that surreal, graphic quality that runs through my work.”

But she isn’t stopping there. Jones has big dreams for the future of her eponymous studio, a “House” built larger with each new release. Next up, she’d like to expand the accessories category further, hoping to add bags, scarves, and other wearable pieces to her lineup.

“I’ve spent years working between art, film, and fashion,” she says. “I’m building towards a collection of things that can accompany you through your life—from what hangs on your wall to what you choose to wear.”

#6852. Nina Katchadourian Spent 11 Years Making Art on Airplanes

The hundreds of photographs are gathered in a new book, Seat Assignment

There are few places where the unwieldiness and malleability of time take center stage quite like when one is on an airplane. It is one of the few times when the world allows for a kind of self-indulgence: You can sleep, catch up on your favorite TV shows, watch a guilty pleasure movie, read a book, or listen to a podcast that you never had time for while on the ground, or you could even raw-dog the flight, just you and your thoughts, getting lost as you hurl through the air at 580 miles per hour inside a hunk of aluminum and other composite materials.

In 2010, the artist Nina Katchadourian was sitting on a plane at LaGuardia Airport in New York on her way to Atlanta, begrudging all of the frequent flying that was keeping her away from her studio and her work. “I thought, ‘Here I am again, about to spend three hours flying somewhere, and it feels as if this time already doesn’t count,’ ” she recalls thinking. “It’s garbage time.” But Katchadourian, whose interdisciplinary approach has led her to work with a wide array of mediums and materials (photography, performance, sculpture, “audio interventions,” a popcorn machine), called herself out. “I thought [it was] a really bad attitude for somebody like me, who claims as an artist to be interested in mundane things and everyday things and believes that things often deserve a closer look.” She challenged herself to “make things the whole way to Atlanta” with whatever she could find around her, photographed with her iPhone. The first “improvisations,” as she called them, included classic airplane objets like pretzels (see them resting comfortably on a king bed), straws (turned into bridges over a mountainscape, and the shiny buckle on the seatbelt (a perfect medium for a self-portrait). “By the time I got to Atlanta, I thought, ‘I need to keep doing this because something just happened and I need to see where it will go.’ ” She’d work on it until 2021, when the pandemic provided a natural pause.

Now the entire project has been published as a small tome appropriately titled Seat Assignment. The hundreds of images are a testament to how curiosity about the world can open and create an alternate universe that lives exactly within the limits of our own, and Katchadourian is open to both the humorous (a little puppet face made from a bread roll with blueberries for eyes and a strawberry tongue, politicians and businessmen with noses and beards made out of pieces of croissants and radishes, a pile of sweater that resembles a gorilla) and the sublime (a Stonehenge-esque architectural formation made from mint Life Savers, mountains dotted with sweater-fuzz black clouds, mysterious trees with twisted-napkin vines that wrap around them). On a particularly long flight from San Francisco to Auckland, New Zealand, she holed up in the lavatory and produced a series of portraits “in the Flemish style,” repurposing toilet-seat covers and paper towels into dramatic collars and hats. The portraits are uncanny, often capturing the masters’ chiaroscuro effect.

She quickly set some ground rules as she embarked on the project: She wouldn’t bring anything to “make” art on the plane, could only work with what she found, and would only use her iPhone to document what she’d made. “I felt like doing [those things] would make everything too easy, and it would make it all too much like ‘art,’ ” she explains. Curiously, during the years she worked on Seat Assignment, the quality of the camera on the iPhone improved so much that it being part of her rules became less important. “In those early days, people would say to me, ‘Well, why aren’t you using a real camera?’ ” she explains. “That question disappeared after a while.”

In a way, it seems like it was only a matter of time before Katchadourian embarked on an airplane project. As a child, she traveled every summer with her parents and younger brother from Northern California, where she grew up, to a small island off the south coast of Finland, where her mother’s family was from. The island was hugely influential on how she saw the world (“I’ve made a lot of artwork there, and I really feel like my approaches to artmaking have been learned there”), but so was the long flight itself. She’d make up games with her brother, trying to guess the movie that would play during the flight (back in the day when there was only one television screen for passengers to watch). On one occasion, her mother brought sheets and blankets and made a bed and pillows for the two young children on the floor beneath their seats. “They would never let you do this now, but I remember when it was my turn to sleep down there, looking to the side and seeing this landscape of legs that went through the whole plane. It was amazing, an underworld of under the seats.” She adds, “The whole plane was a place that we were all temporary residents of, and it wasn’t a place I disliked at all. It was this space that took us from one place I loved to another place I loved.”

This point of view has guided much of her work, like “Sorted Books,” another ongoing project she started in 1993, where she organizes book collections—whether belonging to a friend or an institution—into poems or literary fragments; “The Genealogy of the Supermarket,” which began in 2005, where she organizes all of the “people” that feature in grocery products (Betty Crocker, Orville Redenbacher, the Gerber Baby) into a genealogical tree; and “Mended Spiderwebs,” where she completed spiderwebs with delicate red thread. Though disparate, her projects always invite audiences to truly look at the world around them. “I think for the first 10 years it used to concern me a lot that I never did the same things two times in a row,” Katchadourian says. “After a while, I realized that’s actually a methodology. It’s not a deficit; it’s a way of working. A way of saying the medium is secondary to the approach.”

“I think a lot of projects for me have come out of a ‘what if?’ sort of question that I ask myself, and then I try to sort out an inquiry,” she says. Curiosity and happenstance, or the idea of chance, are essential in Katchadourian’s practice. “The weird thing about research is that you don’t always know that you’re doing it, so sometimes you’re reading or having experiences and only later do you realize, ‘Oh, all that curiosity was actually in service of trying to figure this thing out.’ ” She continues, “I think you have to take diversions seriously because they are showing you something back if you’re willing to observe yourself doing it. There’s something in it that often holds a lot of information.” It’s also what her work does.

#6851. Here’s When Every Episode of ‘The Rings of Power’ Season 3 Drops

Lord of the Rings fans are used to long binges... right? 😰

For many fans, The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power has been leading up to a lot of the events in season 3, which drops this November on Prime Video. We’re actually gonna see sexy Sauron (Charlie Vickers) forge *that* ring. Now, Peter Jackson turned the war between elves, men, orcs, and more that follows into the prologue of his The Fellowship of the Ring movie...but so much more happened than even his Extended Editions didn’t include. His Galadriel (Cate Blanchett) didn’t even mention relationship history with Sauron. Let this be your guide to the very dark, deceitful, and extraordinarily dramatic third season of The Rings of Power.

Funny I should mention the Extended Editions, actually, because watching the Prime Video series this season is going to feel a lot like settling down for a four-hour film every week. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power has decided to release episodes in three “batches” this season. That means that we’ll have multiple episodes to watch every Wednesday, and it’ll be over by December. This is good news for those who have been waiting impatiently for new Middle-earth stories, and terrible news for those of us who like to savor new seasons of television, talk it out with other fans, and experience it together at the same pace. You can’t please everyone!

The premiere is November 11, aka Veterans Day in the United States. And if you haven’t already served, you might feel like you have after binging the four episodes dropping at once on that day. That’s right.

The series is available to watch on Prime Video.

The streamer typically does a midnight release. However, again... let’s think about these batch drops. It might be smarter to log off social media, and wait until the next morning, rather than stay up all night watching the new episodes.

The season will have eight episodes total, just like the two seasons before it. How many episodes will we get each week with this whole batch thing? Like so: four episodes in week 1, two in week 2, and then two in week 3. Got it?

The co-creators J. D. Payne and Patrick McKay alternated writing episodes with writer and producer Justin Noble. Episodes 1, 2, and 8 were directed by Charlotte Brändström, who also directed many episodes in seasons 1 and 2. Episodes 2–4 were directed by Sanaa Hamri, who joined the series in season 2. And episodes 5 and 6 were directed by Stefan Schwartz, who is new to Rings of Power.

#6848. How Marty Supreme Costume Designer Miyako Bellizzi Really Shops

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The consummate downtown girl joins Bazaar’s podcast to talk about collecting vintage skirt suits and Supreme hoodies

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

From Uncut Gems to Marty Supreme, Oscar-nominated costume designer Miyako Bellizzi is responsible for dressing some of the most memorable films of the past decade. A longtime collaborator with the Safdie brothers—Joshua and Benjamin—she’s brought the filmmakers’ stories to life with detail-oriented styling that feels lived-in and real. Whether capturing the ostentatious wardrobe of a 2012 Diamond District hustler or crafting 1950s-accurate ensembles for over 3,000 background actors in the Timothée Chalamet–led ping-pong drama, she’s proven time and time again she has a knack for storytelling.

In her personal life, this expertise has translated into an eclectic closet full of vintage finds. Known for mixing high-end designer pieces with streetwear pulls and 1940s workwear, she dresses herself with as much intention as she does the characters you see on screen. Showing up to film this week’s installment of Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy, she paired a fitted blazer from her collection of 1940s and ’50s skirt suits with shorts she made herself, Phoebe Philo flats, and sculptural silver statement earrings.

“I love costume design, I love fashion, I love film, I love it all,” she said. “I love people, so I think that’s why.”

In the episode, Bellizzi dives into everything from costuming the screen to shopping for herself. Read on for highlights and watch the show in full above.

“I’ve always loved vintage, I think my parents put me onto it. My grandma had all these clothes from New York from the ’50s and ’60s that she didn’t want, and my dad is a big vintage buyer. Even my Barbies were vintage Barbies. But I also just appreciate well-made pieces, I appreciate good fabrics, things that you can’t find today. As a designer, it’s fascinating to see how things were made back in different eras.

“I collect vintage 1940s and ’50s skirt suits. It’s always been my favorite era, which was why Marty Supreme was a dream film for me. I think I started collecting in high school—we used to have vintage fairs in San Francisco, where I’m from. I like the shape and silhouette. This is what Mugler referenced. I’m a historical person and I love the shapes and silhouettes of Mugler, and I have a lot of Mugler, but also I know where it comes from, so I like to go to the source.”

“I don’t personally buy a lot of contemporary brands for myself, other than Phoebe Philo and the Row. I [shop there for] very classic basics, because I find all the freaky weird things vintage.”

“When I was a kid, I was like 5’9” in the fifth grade. I was really tall. And we’d always have to shop in the boy’s section. When I first started, I was at Details Magazine. It was my first internship; that’s why I moved to New York. But I always felt out of place. I never felt like I fit in with the fashion girlies of that time. And so I remember enjoying menswear and learning about menswear and tailoring, and I found that was more inspiring to me than being in the women’s fashion closet.”

“I was at Vice and I was doing all the fashion stories, and there was a mass exodus and it was the group that left that asked if I would be interested in doing a film. I had never done one before, and I was like, ‘Sure, I’ll try.’ Then, [with the Safdie brothers,] we had mutual friends. The film industry is very small. Everything is timing. I never thought I wanted to be a costume designer; this was not the goal when I first moved to New York. But I love it because it’s this family—you travel with your crew and you’re doing these jobs together and you live together. You’re living and breathing this alternate reality. All you’re thinking about are these characters. It’s almost like how an actor has to get into character. You have to become the character you’re creating.”

“It was tough, honestly. You have to think about yourself being presented in that way. Traditionally, most costume designers aren’t fashion girlies. I was excited for it but it was also very stressful. I remember I was working in Paris at the time and I was staying with [designer] Gabe [Semeraro]. I was freaking out. I went to a few vintage archives there, being like, ‘At least I’m in Paris. I can find a dress.’ And I did find a really beautiful red Galliano dress that I loved that I was going to rent, but I was like, ‘I don’t know, it’s so expensive.’ One night, I was home with Gabe, and he was like, ‘Babe, I’m just gonna make one for you.’ It was one of my favorite dresses I’ve ever worn or seen.”

“Every time I wrap a film, it feels like an achievement. I remember I was doing a film in Hawaii, and when I was there, I loved that the women wore their wealth on their wrists. They stack solid gold bangles [on their wrists], and most of times they have names of people in their families [engraved]. And they’re the most beautiful things. I figured out where to find them—at a flea market—and I got one and had it engraved. Now I’ve turned it into a thing where I buy myself a piece of jewelry or something really nice after I wrap a film."

“I am very thoughtful about what I buy for myself. I never want to spend too much money on myself. But I have made a folder on my phone of pieces I regret not buying. We were in Paris and went to Alaïa and I wanted this pink set that I didn’t buy and I still think about it.”

“Now that we are approaching fall, I live in a long sleeve. That’s my Bay area [upbringing] coming out of me. I need to have a XXXL long-sleeve T-shirt, heavy cotton. That is my comfort layer. I bring one everywhere. That and a hoodie. Those are my two hometown pieces. And when I get obsessed with something, I find a lot of them. I have a lot of Supreme hoodies, although my new favorites are 6397, because they have fun colors.”

“I was thinking about what I would buy if I had all the money in the world. It’s a pair of cuffs—or, actually, anything from this jewelry designer Art Smith. It’s kind of modernist jewelry from a different era, like 50 years ago. So something like that. Sophie Buhai is kind of the modern-day equivalent. Pieces that could be in museums.”

“I’m going to be realistic—I did buy four pairs of shoes this week: Chanel flats [from Matthieu Blazy’s first collection] on the RealReal, a pair of [Michael Rider’s] Celine jazz shoes, two pairs of Bottega woven flats.”

#6846. Cosmo’s Acne-Prone Beauty Editors Swear by These Under-$30 Products for Clearer Skin

From makeup-removing balms to bacteria-fighting sprays.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I’ve experienced my fair share of adult acne over the last five years (see: persistent chin pimples and a two-year stint on spironolactone), so I’ve learned just about every skincare hack to keep my breakouts in check. And that you should be ditching those makeup wipes in exchange for a cleansing balm that simultaneously washes your face and has nourishing benefits?

#6844. Are You the Next Iconic Kitchen Designer?

The Monogram Iconic Kitchen Design Awards honor the visionaries redefining luxury kitchen design through exceptional creativity, craftsmanship, and innovation.

An extraordinary kitchen is defined by refined materials, exquisite details, and design that balances ease with sophistication. These spaces do more than serve a purpose; they elevate how we live. For designers who share this philosophy, the prestigious Monogram Iconic Kitchen Awards are built to recognize your vision.

Now through November 30, architects, interior designers, builders, and remodelers can submit their completed projects featuring Monogram appliances for the honor of industry recognition, features in ELLE Decor, House Beautiful, and Veranda, and cash prizes of up to $25,000.

“The Monogram Iconic Kitchen Awards are rooted in our belief that the kitchen is both a functional space and a deeply personal expression of how people live,” says Julie Burns, executive director of Monogram. “Through this program, we’re proud to celebrate the designers who push the boundaries of luxury kitchen design while creating spaces that are as livable as they are beautiful.”

The awards will recognize 12 finalists in four categories, with one winner in each. Categories include:

Burns notes that the Monogram Iconic Kitchen Design Awards identify work that “continues to raise the bar for luxury living while inspiring the future of kitchen design.” An esteemed panel of judges with a discerning eye for luxury design will evaluate the submissions based on five key elements: the thoughtful integration of Monogram in the design, design excellence, functionality and space planning, innovation, and originality, as well as the impact of the design statement and imagery.

Twelve finalists will be celebrated in February at the Kitchen & Bath Industry Show (KBIS) and winners will be announced during an exclusive celebration at the High Point Market in April. Each category winner will receive $25,000 and media recognition; first runner-ups will win $10,000, and second runner-ups $5,000. All 12 finalists will be invited to attend the High Point Market celebration, including travel, accommodations, and event access.

Entrants are required to submit floor plans or layout drawings, elevation or renderings, completed project photography, and a written design statement describing the project’s vision, inspiration, and what makes it iconic, along with details on the appliances used in the space and other personal and project information.

What makes a kitchen iconic? Exceptional design is more than what you see; it’s how a space makes you feel. An iconic kitchen represents the perfect harmony of aesthetic beauty and quiet purpose, crafted to leave a lasting impression.

We invite you to review the submission guidelines and share your outstanding achievements in luxury design on the Monogram Iconic Kitchen Design Awards page.

#6842. Is There a Hookup to Relationship Pipeline?

Yes, it’s true: my relationship started off as a hookup. My boyfriend—who I now love, am fully committed to, and have way too much fun in Costco with—used to send me 2AM “WYD” texts and avoided any sort of conversation about the future.

Yes, I made it out of the situationship trenches. I’ll hold for applause.

While I would like to pretend that I’m better than everyone else, the reality is that there is no secret method to turning your situationship into a real relationship. Sometimes—like in my case—it’s just science.

A 2018 study revealed that sexual desire can actually encourage people to engage in behaviors that help initiate romantic relationships. Through different activities like slow dancing, researchers found that sexual attraction made people want to get physically closer, mirror each other’s behaviors, become more emotionally responsive, and even become more helpful.

We often hear of relationships that begin with emotional connection and then turn into something more romantic (and eventually physical), but the other side of the coin exists—and I’m proof of it.

“Sometimes physical attraction is what initially brings two people together, and that attraction creates opportunities to spend more time together, get to know each other, and potentially discover a deeper emotional connection,” licensed psychologist Dr Matthew Leahy tells Cosmopolitan. “What starts as something purely physical can open the door to something much more meaningful.”

I started hooking up with my now-partner purely because he was hot. Turns out, he was great in bed, too. And because we enjoyed having sex so much, we just ended up spending a lot of time together—so that we could have more of said sex, of course. Eventually, in between water breaks and refueling with Uber Eats orders, we got to know each other. I remember being shocked at what a great guy he ended up being—you know, when we actually took the time to get off each other and talk. Eventually, the booty calls turned into regular phone calls, and late night hookups turned into week-long sleepovers.

But our romantic relationship wasn’t inevitable just because we were sexually attracted to each other. We had to have a lot of conversations about our values, our hopes, and most importantly, what we wanted out of a relationship.

There were also a lot of check-ins I had to do with myself. The sex was great, so I had to constantly make sure I wasn’t confusing a sexual connection for an emotional one (AKA being “dickmatized”). Dr Leahy explains that one way to do this is to ask yourself objective questions about the other person: Are they asking you questions? Is the communication strong? Are they paying attention to you? Do you have things in common besides sexual desires?

“I think it’s good to always take a step back, have some self-awareness, and truly ask yourself: Is this going somewhere? Do I want it to go somewhere, or am I OK with it the way it is?”

Coming to terms with the reality of the situation is hard, but the hardest part of it all? You have to actually put yourself out there—especially if you really want to be with that person.

“Communicating how you truly feel about somebody and what you’re looking for can happen at any time in a relationship,” Dr. Leahy says. But when you know you’re falling hard for someone, the sooner you express those things the better.

I think it’s important to take a beat and remind you that developing feelings for someone you’re constantly being sexually intimate with is totally normal. I know we’re doomed to a dating era of nonchalance, but there’s nothing wrong with liking someone you’re exchanging bodily fluids with—especially if you’re spending a lot of time together.

You just have to know the difference between when your hookup is veering into a romantic connection and when it’s purely physical.

Before you send this article to your situationship, there’s a reality check that needs to happen. Movies like Friends With Benefits and No Strings Attached made us believe that any situation where we’re hooking up with someone we like as a person is bound to lead to us falling in love. I’m sorry to tell you this isn’t true, and he won’t be waiting for you with a flashmob.

I had situationships before my current boyfriend that went nowhere but down the toilet. While the sexual chemistry was there, nothing else quite was.

So how do you tell when something is just a hookup versus the potential of something more?

“It’s important to pay attention to how the other person responds when you communicate what you’re looking for,” Dr. Leahy says.

My partner and I actually stopped talking for a while after I first initiated a conversation about wanting more than just sex. He was very honest with me and said that he wasn’t in the place in his life to pursue a commitment. I had my answer and could decide what I wanted to do with that information. Knowing that I couldn’t just continue to hook up with him without my feelings getting complicated, I cut things off.

“If you’ve been clear about wanting something more and the other person isn’t showing that same interest, you have to be willing to recognize that,” Dr. Leahy says.

We went our separate ways and grew as people. Years later, we reconnected and found ourselves on the same page.

While I’d love to tell you that this is how it always works out in the end—it’s simply not true. Unfortunately, sometimes no matter how much we like someone—despite having intense sexual chemistry—nothing comes of it. Even if you both do have romantic feelings, you both need to be in a place where you want to pursue a relationship. And had I let myself linger in the liminal space of hooking up with someone I had romantic feelings for, I wouldn’t even be in the right headspace to be in the healthy relationship I’m in now—even if it was with someone different.

“Sometimes we get caught up in hoping a relationship will become something different, rather than accepting what it actually is,” Dr. Leahy says. “You can’t convince somebody to want the same kind of relationship you do, and that’s an important part of knowing when it might be time to move on.”

So if you’re currently hooking up with someone you’re starting to develop feelings for, maybe it’s time to have a talk with yourself, and then them. You’ll either end up in a relationship, or better off for knowing that they don’t feel the same way.

#6833. Should We Be Worried About How Much Sex Married People Are Having?

Does saying “I do” mean saying “I don’t” to sex?

Stand-up comedian Greg Fitzsimmons famously said, “If you want to stop two people from having sex, let them get married.” Turns out, he’s not that off base.

According to the recent General Social Survey, 32.5 percent of married U.S. adults said they haven’t had sex “at all” in the last 12 months. That means that almost 1 in 3 married people aren’t really having sex with their spouses. I knew that the state of dating was bleak and that Gen Z was having less and less sex, but I wasn’t aware that this issue of lack of intimacy was prevalent in marriage too.

While it’s important to note that sexual frequency doesn’t give us a complete picture of what’s going on in a relationship, there is still something to be said about the state of sex in this country. So what’s going on in the bedrooms of married couples?

Ilana Grines, LMFT and licensed sex therapist, shares that she often hears couples say that they’re exhausted by the end of the day.

“Between shuffling children, work, and other responsibilities, sex frequently takes the back seat and is deprioritized,” she says. And because couples spend so much of their time mentally occupied by their to-do lists, there’s not a lot of time (or emotional energy) left over to transition into a sensual state. Intimacy requires a lot of mental and emotional space—which can be hard to tap into if you’re on “go” from the moment you wake up to the moment you go to bed.

Plus, there are a lot of mental hangups that get in the way of sex. Grines says that unresolved resentments have a lot to do with this. In heterosexual relationships, women take on a majority of the household and childcare labor. We’re often told that “foreplay starts at breakfast.” Well, if you’re the one cooking breakfast alone, feeding it to your spouse and kids, cleaning up afterwards, and then spending your entire day tending to a majority of the household tasks thinking about how your partner is pretty useless, it’s hard to be enthusiastic about getting into bed with them.

Now, some changes in your sex life are just inevitable. There’s work stress, medication changes, pain, and moving through different life stages. These things, Grines says, can cause shifts in desire.

“These ebbs may be met with a decrease in sexual activity, but do not typically include a change in affection, warmth, and trust,” she says.

But how a partner shows up during these times does affect the relationship. If a partner is unkind, unhelpful, or detached during life changes, it can result in the other partner being closed off to sex or any kind of intimacy.

How do you both open up (literally and figuratively) again?

“A good way to nurture a long term sexual relationship is to understand what conditions are required for desire to exist in the first place,” Grines says. The first step is to get curious.

If you notice your partner pull away—or notice yourself pulling away—it’s important to try to understand what’s happening in your relationship or your own life that may be contributing to this change. Grines suggests posing questions like, “What do we do that allows us to make sex more inviting?” “Are we always just leaving it to be the last item on our to-do list.. or prioritizing it?,” and “Do we need to start scheduling time to just be intimate, without the pressure of having sex?”

By making these questions part of a regular relationship check in, you and your partner can mentally make a note that sex is an important part of your relationship.

“It is also really important to redefine what it means to have sex,” Grines says. She explains that some couples think penetration is the only way to maintain an intimate connection. But there’s affectionate touch, cuddling, kissing, lap-sitting, and basically any kind of touch that can help create a sense of connection without the pressure of full-blown sex.

And of course, you have to get to the root of the issue. No matter how much you cuddle with your partner, if there is a feeling of resentment around your relationship, then this needs to be addressed. Intimate touch is not a bandaid for the relational issues you and your partner are facing. You’re going to have to either talk it out or seek the help of a professional to guide the conversation. Yes, sex is important for most relationships—but the actual relationship part should be a priority.

If you’re worried that saying “I do” is going to automatically mean you’re never going to have sex again, relax. Marriage is not some death sentence for your sex life. But it will take some work to help create a sexual connection with your partner that withstands all of the change that marriage (or any serious, longterm relationship) brings.

“New relationship energy comes with anticipation, separateness, novelty, and a lot of intentional time devoted to each other,” Grines says. “That intentionality often fades in long term relationships which often contributes to the lessening of sexual intimacy.”

“Change over time happens for many couples, but losing intimacy altogether is far from inevitable,” she says. “Couples can not control every factor that influences desire, but they can influence and understand which conditions exist that make desire more likely to exist.” For you and your partner, this may look like spending uninterrupted time together, being playful, addressing any issues as they arise, working to create an equal partnership, and constantly talking about what pleasure and connection means to the both of you.

The earlier in the relationship you talk about sex, the better chance you have at preserving a sexual connection throughout it.

Grines encourages couples to refrain from measuring themselves against some invisible standard when it comes to sex. Sure, you might want to have sex more than once in a year—but maybe you don’t need it all the time. And there’s nothing wrong with that.

“Focus on creating a satisfying sex life to the parties involved,” Grines says. “Pne that is able to be discussed and adapted together as their life changes.”

#6819. Margot Robbie’s Side-Parted ‘Buttercup Bob’ Is Polished and Easy to Wear: How to Try It Now

The actor and producer proves how a simple styling switch can make all the difference.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are few hair updates as simple, yet as impactful, as switching up your part. After several seasons dominated by center-parted lengths and deliberately undone styling, the side part is making a convincing return. The latest to make a case for its appeal? None other than Margot Robbie.

Attending Chanel’s Cruise 2026/2027 show in Australia, the actor and producer stepped out with a softly structured ‘buttercup bob,’ styled with a deep side part and flipped-out ends. The result was equal parts polished and quietly sophisticated. And while Robbie has recently been favoring the waterfall bob, this latest iteration feels notably sleeker, offering a more refined take on the trending cut.

“This bob is an effortless take on a classic jaw-length cut. It’s neat without feeling rigid, with soft ends and a clean shape that gives hair an expensive-looking finish,” Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons previously told ELLE. In short, it trades lived-in texture for a more polished silhouette, with precise ends and subtle flicks upward that create a sharper finish.

Robbie’s latest style embodies the look perfectly, pairing a side part with softly sculpted ends for an updated take on the preppy bob. The part allows for a sweeping side bang, adding a touch of glamour to an otherwise classic cut.

When it comes to re-creating Robbie’s look, Talia Hurley, a stylist at Blue Tit London, recommends blow-drying the hair with a round brush for a glossy, classic finish. This will also help create added volume and the bend needed to achieve the look’s side-swept shape.

Luke Hersheson recommends finishing the look with a pea-sized amount of smoothing cream to add shine and tame flyaways without leaving the hair greasy, ensuring the style retains its polished, glossy finish.

#6817. Hanifa Is Back: Inside the Return of the Black-Owned Luxury Fashion Brand After an Eight-Month Pause

After eight months away, Anifa Mvuemba reflects on the decision to pause and shares what's ahead.

“Is that Hanifa?” is a question I got often walking down any street, from Miami, New York, to D.C. and Los Angeles. The clothing brand built a cult following over the years, largely made of career-focused, fashion-obsessed Black women around the world who loved existing as a “Hanifa woman.” Anifa Mvuemba originally launched the label in 2012, initially gaining traction after she posted a self-made 21st-birthday dress on Instagram. The brand slowly continued to grow, and then eventually blew up on a widespread global stage during the COVID-19 pandemic when it launched its groundbreaking “Pink Label Congo” collection via a viral 3D virtual runway using headless digital avatars.

The independent brand became synonymous with luxury, sensuality, and well-made designs. But that all came to a halt this past March when Mvuemba announced a pause for the brand due to an assortment of backend issues—from shipping delays, warehouse complications, and burnout.

But after taking time for herself and resetting, Mvuemba is entering a new chapter. And Hanifa is set to return November 18, 2026. We caught up with the 35-year-old founder to talk about her time away and what shoppers can expect now that Hanifa is coming back.

Honestly, Hanifa is like a part of my DNA at this point. I started this brand when I was 21, and so I think I just needed a moment to just take a breather, rest, be with my new baby, enjoy my family, enjoy this time, and just think about how I wanted to move forward. It was a lot, but eventually I was just like, ‘Okay, I need to get back to work,’ because I genuinely enjoy and love what I do, creating. I'm such a creative person, and I love business. I can't leave a Hanifa alone like that. I can't do it.

I really was thinking about the opportunity that this pause awarded us. It gave us the opportunity to build this dream world of Hanifa that I've always wanted to have. To be completely honest, within the last couple of years, I always wanted to have a moment to fix things, rebrand a few things, and I felt like I finally got the opportunity to be able to do that without feeling the pressure of doing all of that. So we were able to go back into our archives. I really wanted to start there, and think about the woman that we are building for. Just looking at those moments that the customers fell in love with from the beginning, and reintroducing that in different ways. Of course, keeping the woman, her body, at the center. I wanted for this new era for the brand in design to feel a little bit more elevated, a little refined, while still remaining true to Hanifa. And then just exploring different things. We really wanted to try different materials. Knitwear being our biggest category, wanting to explore different ways that we can do knitwear, color, textures, fits, patterns, while still staying true to the brand and what people love us for.

I really understood the importance of resting. I'm someone that enjoys working, but I think rest is actually necessary to be able to have peace of mind. I thought it was necessary for me to be present for my family, also for me to be the best leader that I could be for my team, and I think it's okay to rest. I'm grateful that we were able to even pause for this long. It's not usually the norm for independent brands. But I'm grateful that we even had that opportunity to be able to say, “Hey, we're gonna step away for eight months, 10 months, and just figure it out and do all the things that we needed to do.”

I'm excited for people to experience this new era of Hanifa. We've been working so hard, considering every touchpoint for the customer, restructuring the business. I mean, we've literally been uprooting everything, and refining, going back and forth and redoing the website. I'm very, very excited for everyone to really experience this world that we're building. And so the collection is called World of Anifa. We're now allowing them into this space and making the brand feel more full. We always speak about how we've grown with our customers, we're journeying with our customers, and wanting them to experience what it is to be in this world, and how we actually consider them in the decisions we make too.

She's evolving. I think we're all evolving. I was 21 when I started, my perspective was different. Now I'm turning 36 this year, and everything is shifting. So she's grown with us. We've grown with her. She's rediscovering herself. She's maybe a new mom. Maybe she just got her new executive position. Maybe she just started a business. She's in a new season in her life, and so she still wants to feel seen. She still wants to look good. Also a big thing that we're building is the categories, really diversifying that so that she can really have Hanifa all the time. Kid drop-offs, airport outfit. You know, we're thinking about every single detail.

I don't care. Period. But seriously, it took a while for me to get to this space. I do care actually because I see it affecting my peers and people that I care about, too. When I announced the pause, I received so many DMs, messages, and support from other women who own businesses, Black women, too, who shared what they've experienced, similar situations of what we went through. And I realized that there really isn't a safe space for us in this world, and so that's also something that I'm working on on the back end, figuring out how we can have these safe spaces for founders like me, where we can lean on each other, support each other, because I think we'll just be stronger as a unit. Literally, a friend of mine reached out to me recently. Was just like, “Oh my god, this is going on.” I'm just like, “Here's what to do. Here's what you could do.” Being able to support each other in that way, I think that's the most important thing because you really can't focus on the noise. I think that was the hardest thing: just type it, delete it. Respond and delete your response. But at the end of the day, the algorithm makes you feel like everyone is talking about it when in reality they're not. So you just kind of have to take it for what it is and move on.

I think in this new era, I really want to focus on educating the customer and just letting them know what it is, and figuring out the best way that we can talk about what goes into building these brands that we have and building this world and letting them in, giving them a little bit more of that insight, so that there's more of an understanding. Because even with the chatter that happened earlier this year, as I was just processing and reflecting and reviewing, I was like, “There's also a lot of like misinformation,” and I'm like, “I don't even know if they really even understand.” We want to make sure we have an educational component within that so that we can make sure that that's clear because the girls need to know.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6816. Designers Say These Are the Best Colors to Pair With Blue in Any Room

They perfectly complement the rainbow's most versatile hue.

A designer-favorite color to use in the home is beautiful, calming blue. Evoking natural elements like the sea and the sky, this serene hue can make any room feel more relaxed and welcoming, yet also more vibrant and bold, depending on the shades. And if this versatile hue is one you’re thinking about adding to your home, check out these beautiful colors that go with blue, proven by these designer spaces.

No matter your design style, this list of 38 colors that go with blue serves as evidence that the hue is as versatile as a neutral and can elevate any space you use it in. Read on to find your new favorite color scheme here.

To contrast the fanciful chinoiserie wallpaper in this San Francisco apartment, interior designer Caitlin Jones Ghajar and her team went bold and chose a warm yellow for the velvet bed frame. “This particular kind of blue lends itself really well to the vistas that are beyond, so you get some Bay view looks,” she says.

In this Amber Lewis–designed kitchen, the duck egg blue cabinetry is grounded by the rust red and cream floor tile. The hue, Light Blue by Farrow & Ball, can lean almost gray in soft lighting, making it a versatile neutral ready to pair with warm tones.

The deep, black accents in this kitchen add drama and depth to the slate blue cabinetry. The graphic color pairing is modern and masculine, while not trying to compete with the lush nature outdoors. Melissa Anderson of OAD Interiors designed this immersive space in Woodstock, New York.

Blue and yellow is a classic and cheerful color pairing. Elevate the duo by choosing a hue with a slightly greener tone, like a chartreuse. In this guest room by French & French, the cool light blue prevents a too-neon appearance.

Pairing a cool tone with a warm tone is a failsafe approach, and this bedroom by Serena Dugan does it expertly. A golden tan wallpaper softens the vibrant indigo accents on the headboard and window treatments. If you’re feeling bold, throw in some orange for added impact.

When you're unsure of what colors go with blue the best, simply opt for more of the same, as French & French did here. Perfect pattern play is achieved in this lounge through the matching blue and white palettes. A cobalt blue wall ties it all together.

For an impactful palette, add some glitz to your shade of blue. This living room, designed by Suzanne Kasler, utilizes metallics like brass and bronze to elevate the everyday hue to glamorous new heights.

In this Santa Fe home designed by the duo behind French & French, a rich, terra-cotta red balances the vibrant blue tones. To ground the swirling marbled wallpaper on the ceiling, the grand bookcases and coordinating rug (and sneaky chair piping!) incorporate the hue throughout the entire space.

In House Beautiful’s 2024 Whole Home, the guest room is cocooned in a mustard and navy color palette. Blue and yellow primary colors play off one another for a vibrant interior. Use neutral bedding to ground the bright shades.

Pink and blue can often feel like a baby shower or gender reveal, but take inspiration from designer Dane Austin to know how to use them correctly. By combining a pastel with a more vibrant tone, you can elevate the color pairing from juvenile to sophisticated.

Look to nature for hues to use with blue. This chic powder room, complete with a navy blue sink, is swathed in a floral green wallpaper. This color pairing is cheerful and can brighten up a smaller space, especially this one by Liz Carroll, which lacks windows.

Adorned in shades of cobalt and navy, the primary bedroom in our 2024 Whole Home in Asheville, North Carolina, continues the moody color palette with an ochre wallpaper. The hues are simultaneously subdued yet impactful, ideal for a serene night’s sleep.

A spa-like and timeless blue and gray color palette can brighten even the dingiest basements. To ensure nothing looks out of place, keep both hues at the same vibrancy level (e.g., a light blue and a light gray).

To add drama to this teal bedroom, designer Lisa Tharp opted for a totally red upholstered bed frame, and somehow it works. Blue and red are both primary colors, which means they pair well when one starts to lean toward the other’s complement (like the slightly green-toned blue walls).

In this playful kids' bathroom, a bright blue is the focal point color in the space. Looking to the color wheel for a clue, homeowner Ashley Maddox and designer Hilary Walker opted for a burnt orange shower curtain as the accent hue.

Pairing a myriad of cool-toned colors can create a sophisticated palette when done well, like in this Boston apartment. A purple rug complements the blue sectional sofa because they have similar textiles, tones, and brightness levels.

If you’re a fan of the blue and yellow primary pair, you’ll love this elevated option. Pairing a deep, rich blue, like this navy kitchen, with a metallic brass accent is a great way to add some glamour and texture to your space.

Monochrome color palettes can be really impactful when done with various shades of the same hue. Take this living room by Mark D. Sikes for example. The deep blue upholstery on the sofa and ottoman ground the lighter blue used to color drench the walls and ceiling.

Avery Cox of Avery Cox Design used a tonal blue for the wall to give this room an immersive effect and paired it with pops of bubblegum pink, purple, and citron yellow. The colors are the perfect pick-me-up when you're lounging indoors on a rainy day.

This robin's egg blue wallpaper is speckled with ivory and forest green to make daydreaming easy. The ivory mirror and green accessories designer Shannon Eddings added to this dining room are the supporting characters that soften the blue so it doesn't overwhelm the space.

#6812. Sweet Dreams Are Made of Silk: Shop the Best Pajamas for a Luxurious Night’s Sleep

From washable silk sets to breezy nightdresses, these are the pairs worth slipping into.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Listen, some people sleep happily in cotton shorts and a T-shirt, and some of us love slipping into a pair of luxurious silk pajamas. I’m honestly into both, but there’s something about slippery silk PJs that makes getting ready for bed even more appealing.

Bedtime is deeply personal, which is why there’s a silk pajama for every preference. Whether you like pants and long sleeves, a flowing nightdress, or a shorts-and-cami set, there’s a silk version of each to shop. And now that washable silk is an option, investing in a silk sleep set is far more enticing.

Of course, the best set is the one you’ll actually reach for. And one that maybe you’ll even want to wear out from time to time. If you run warm, silk’s natural breathability makes shorts or a cami set a smart pick for summer nights, while long sleeves and pants keep you cozy once the temperature drops, since silk adapts to the weather. A nightdress is ideal if you can’t stand the feeling of something twisting around your legs at 2 a.m., and a classic button-up set is the one to choose if you want to feel put together from the first sip of coffee to the final page of your book.

#6807. Every Outfit TV Is the Digital-Age Fashion Police We Never Knew We Needed

The founders of @everyoutfitonSATC spill the tea on their latest venture into video podcasting

“There are some things that need to be seen to be believed,” says Lauren Garroni, the brunette half of @EveryOutfitonSATC—a Sex and the City fashion archive Instagram account turned Fashion Police-style podcast that she runs with her blonde counterpart, Chelsea Fairless. She’s talking about a stretched canvas image of Dave Navarro walking in the Anna Sui Spring-Summer 1997 runway show, which is currently being sold online by a well-known publishing company. But she might as well have been speaking about the pair’s latest venture: Every Outfit TV.

“Every Outfit is a weekly rundown of the comings and goings in pop culture and fashion,” explains Garroni. She and Fairless launched their podcast in 2021, around the time that the SATC spinoff And Just Like That was announced. Devotees will recall that it was actually phase two, phase one being an Instagram account born in 2016 from Fairless’s frustration with the lack of variety when it came to photos of SATC outfits on the internet. Ten years later, the podcast is viral, the Instagram has 709k followers, and the overall project has morphed into Every Outfit TV, a video component available exclusively on Patreon.

The video format offers Fairless and Garroni the chance to do things that wouldn’t work with solely audio. (Note: not every new podcast episode will have video.) “We grew up being obsessed with the VH1 Vogue Fashion Awards and stuff like House of Style,” says Fairless. The brilliantly lo-fi, green-screen-style opening sequence posted to their Instagram account last week distills the energy they’re going for with its nods to The Nanny, Clueless, and Access Hollywood.

Podcast listeners, TV viewers, and Instagram followers can expect new franchises like “It’s Always Fashion Week Somewhere” and “The Runway Rewind,” which will showcase the pair’s thoughts on runway shows while tapping into the tabloid entertainment vibe that they both admire. There will also be Oprah Winfrey-style book club episodes and “Top 10” lists fiercely curated by Garroni and Fairless themselves.

Ask the duo why now was the ideal time to pivot, and Fairless answers bluntly. “Lauren forced me to,” she says. Garroni sees it differently. “Chelsea finally succumbed to being on camera,” she refutes. Really, the two are responding to a general podcast-world shift to video, a medium that enables them to “fulfill an itch,” says Fairless. She didn’t have cable in her house as a child, prompting her to develop an obsession with fashion magazines and websites like First View (RIP). Garroni’s upbringing makes up for it. “My parents just gave me unrestricted access to television, cable, and the internet from a young age,” she says. Now, both of them are self-diagnosed as chronically online and cite hours on TikTok and Instagram as the reason why they collectively possess the knowledge of someone who has a PhD in fashion history.

While this wealth of niche information could potentially alienate an audience, Garroni and Fairless’s playful banter (think Siskel and Ebert, or Waldorf and Statler) makes up for it. They don’t shy away from cross-generational references, either. “We have laundered our older gay men references that we learned from watching E! News and E! True Hollywood Story,” says Garroni.

In the end, the duo intends to be authentically themselves. “I think Lauren and I are examples of millennials who are very culturally Gen X,” says Fairless. “We were always more interested in Gen X things, Gen X people. I didn’t give a shit about the Backstreet Boys when I was a teenager. We were into Hole and Pavement.”

It would be remiss not to talk about the hit television series that started it all. At this point, they have documented every outfit on Sex and the City more than once in the past ten years, but developments in the show’s universe kept them going. “Something would always happen to prevent us from abandoning this,” says Fairless. Garroni agrees, recalling when Cynthia Nixon ran for governor of New York in 2018. (They released a satirical book We Should All Be Mirandas a year later. Garroni claims to be a Miranda with a Carrie rising, while Fairless is a Miranda with a Samantha rising.) “It always sustained us,” says Garroni. “Every moment we thought, ‘Okay, how long can this joke go on for?’”

If Fairless has anything to do with it, the answer to that question is forever. “Sex and the City is never done. If there is one thing I know to be true in this life, it will never go away,” she says. “It will always change forms and there will always be something newsworthy to talk about.”

Asked what else is coming down the pipeline, Garroni is quick to mention field pieces that will take them out of their impeccably designed Los Angeles set, complete with vintage gray furniture designed in the ’90s by architect David Chipperfield and a pink carpet. (It was modeled after a mashup of Halston’s New York City apartment—an Every Outfit hyperfixation—and the Chicago department store Carson Pirie Scott.) Fairless, however, has other goals in mind. She’d like to add more single-breasted blazers to her wardrobe rotation, she says. Also: “I want hair and makeup.”
