#6806. Nicole Kidman’s Long ‘Almond Cream’ Nails Are the Sophisticated Go-With-Everything Trend to Try Now: Get the Look

This manicure proves long almond nails never go out of style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are certain manicures that never go out of style, regardless of the season. Case in point: Nicole Kidman’s long ‘almond cream’ nails. Spotted at the LACMA Art+Film Gala wearing softly tapered almond nails in a glossy milky shade, the actor’s manicure served as a reminder that winter manicures needn’t be dark or glittery to make an impact.

“Kidman’s nails are a great example of how to wear a longer length without them looking too dramatic,” says Susan Carroll, founder of Young LDN. “The almond shape is one of my favorites because it’s incredibly flattering on the hands. The sides taper in slightly before finishing in a soft, rounded point, which helps elongate the fingers and gives a much more delicate appearance than a square or coffin shape.”

Serving as an ideal entry point for anyone looking to experiment with longer nails without committing to a dramatic stiletto shape, the almond silhouette offers a more wearable alternative. Carroll also recommends adapting the length to suit your lifestyle and comfort level. The appeal lies in its versatility, as it can be easily achieved without the need for extensions. “The shape does a lot of the work, and keeping the color subtle makes the overall look feel much more elegant,” she adds.

Re-creating the look is very straightforward, as the key lies in the shape. Ask your nail technician for a soft almond silhouette with rounded tips, rather than anything too sharply pointed. “The key is making sure the nails are evenly shaped and the cuticles are tidy, as there’s nowhere to hide imperfections with such a simple manicure,” says Carroll. To finish, simply apply two coats of a sheer milky beige polish before sealing with a glossy top coat for a light-reflecting finish.

The result is an effortlessly elegant manicure that feels equal parts timeless and wearable.

#6805. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.

#6803. The 6 Winter Coat Trends Defining the Season

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From sculptural tailoring to vintage-inspired faux fur, these are the styles to know

A great winter outfit deserves an equally great coat, especially when it’s an essential layer for the next several months. Beyond the practical need for warmth, outerwear often sets the tone for an entire look as the first layer you see. A sharply tailored wool coat can make jeans and a sweater feel more polished, while an oversize silhouette or rich texture brings dimension and new proportions to otherwise clean-lined looks.

For fall/winter 2026, designers are rethinking classic outerwear through distinctive silhouettes, textures, and details. At Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi, precise tailoring took a dressier turn, with slim silhouettes, tuxedo-inspired designs, and satin lapels bringing eveningwear polish to wool coats. Meanwhile, Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry embraced leather for a sleeker city-ready take on winter dressing. Faux fur took on a distinctly vintage feel at Stella McCartney, Bottega Veneta, and Khaite, while plush collars added texture to everything from elegant overcoats to more everyday designs.

Whether you’re looking to invest in a forever piece or simply want something fresher than the coat you’ve worn for the past few winters, these six trends offer plenty of inspiration.

Faux fur is taking a distinctly retro turn this winter, with plush textures and elegant silhouettes reminiscent of vintage finds. Stella McCartney’s floor-grazing belted coat and Bottega Veneta’s voluminous fur both recall the glamour of the ’30s and ’40s, while Khaite’s shaggy cream jacket has more of a ’70s feel, particularly when dressed down with jeans. Of course, we love a faux-fur coat as the finishing touch to a dressy outfit, but there’s something even chicer about pairing one with everyday denim and boots.

Arma Verena Reversible Shearling and Leather Coat

For fall/winter 2026, the black evening coat is having a moment. Designers showcased styles with luxe updates to keep things more interesting. Think sharp, menswear-inspired tailoring with a more formal finish, as seen at Celine, to elegant satin coats at Dries Van Noten. It’s the go-to coat to wear to upcoming holiday parties and formal dinners, or if you just want to upgrade your traditional winter coat for something a bit more luxurious this season.

Polo Ralph Lauren Wool and Silk Crepe Tuxedo Duster Coat

The Row Erdene Single-Breasted Wool Long Coat

There’s a certain refinement to a beautifully tailored coat, especially when the details borrow from formalwear. Think precise cuts, elongated silhouettes, and tuxedo-inspired touches that feel polished enough for an evening out. There’s a more dressed-up sensibility to these classic designs—as seen at Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi—from the precise fit to the smallest finishing details. This attention to detail brings a beautiful sense of sophistication to workwear and when paired with simple everyday separates.

Gap x Victoria Beckham Wool-Blend Chesterfield Coat

Mackage Elodie-R Double-Breasted Wool-Blend Coat

When a puffer or classic wool coat feels too predictable, a leather coat offers a much bolder alternative. Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry all embraced waist-defining silhouettes, from classic double-breasted trenches to softer robe-like shapes. In dramatic maxi lengths, the effect is especially sleek, with just the right amount of edge, bringing a bit of bite to winter dressing.

Nili Lotan Arsene Leather Double-Breasted Trench Coat

The Frankie Shop Edra Shearling-Trimmed Wrap Coat

Gray was ubiquitous on runways from New York to Paris, appearing in everything from textured tweed to draped wool and sharply tailored shapes. Whether you prefer deep charcoal or soft dove, it’s an easy neutral to wear in tonal layers or to balance bright colors and prints.

Banana Republic Herringbone Wool-Blend Top Coat

Marant Etoile Pilda Herringbone Double-Breasted Coat

’S Max Mara Scarf-Detail Virgin-Wool Wrap Coat

Faux fur was all over the fall/winter 2026 runways, even in smaller doses like plush collars. If you know that feeling of a sharp chill hitting your neck when the rest of you is bundled up, these collars offer the warmth you need in an ultra-elegant way. On everything from sporty puffers and classic toggle-front wool coats, as seen at Louis Vuitton, to the patterned and pretty pastel trims at Chanel and Gabriela Hearst, it’s a detail that’s as practical as it is beautiful.

Max Mara Medusa Belted Puffer Coat with Faux-Fur Trim

#6798. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

#6797. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6796. All About Anthony De La Torre, Lana Condor’s Actor/Musician Husband

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In the To All the Boys films, Lana Condor’s character is often consumed by relationship drama. In any given movie, Lara Jean may be torn between her current boyfriend Peter Kavinsky (Noah Centineo) and childhood crush John Ambrose McClaren (Jordan Fisher), or struggling with whether to follow Peter to college. But in real life, Condor faces no such dilemma. She’s been in a relationship with actor/musician Anthony De La Torre since 2015. In January 2022, the two got engaged, and in October 2024, they got married. Below, all you need to know about De La Torre and his romance with Condor.

Like Condor, De La Torre is an actor who most famously played a young Jack Sparrow in 2017’s Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales. He’s also appeared in episodes of the Nickelodeon series 100 Things to Do Before High School and the Starz show Vida.

The couple actually met at an entertainment industry function. During her February 2019 cover story with Cosmopolitan, Condor spoke about meeting De La Torre at an Emmy nominees reception in 2015. His pickup line? “Hi, I’m Anthony. I thought I would make a friend.” She described their initial meeting to the outlet as being “like a ­Taylor Swift song.”

Ever since their storybook meeting, De La Torre and Condor have stayed by each other’s side. The actress told Cosmopolitan UK that her beau is quite the romantic. “He writes me love letters all the time,” she told the outlet. “He’s the freaking best. He’ll leave me little messages taped to my bathroom mirror on days I have to wake up early for work. He once picked up my favorite takeout food after work and served it up properly on our kitchen table, with little sticky notes attached to each plate saying how proud of me he is.”

Both Condor and De La Torre wrote romantic Instagram posts to celebrate their fifth anniversary as a couple. In August 2020, she shared a photo of the pair cuddled up on the grass with the caption, “5 years with the man of my dreams. You are still my most favorite person in the world. You are my best friend. You have taught me how to love and to be loved, to forgive and to grow, to never give up, to be each others teammate and cheerleader. You make me laugh like no other and it doesn’t matter where we are in the world, as long as I’m with you, I’m home. I love you @anthonydltorre happy anniversary. Let’s cuddle. 🐶”

De La Torre’s post, also shared on August 31, includes a sunset picture. He wrote, “5 years ago we went on our first date and saw a movie called The Gift. While the plot of the film in no way represents our life, the name of the movie couldn’t be more fitting to what you are. The greatest gift I've ever been blessed and entrusted with. From our tiny ass apartment with a blow up twin mattress to riding horses in Kenya, it has always been an adventure 🤪 Thank you for changing my life, inspiring me to grow, and making everything worth it. I love you, my tiny burrito 🌯♥️”

Condor shared the news that De La Torre popped the question on Jan. 28, 2022. She explained the details behind the ring and shared engagement photos, which you can view here on her Instagram.

She wrote, of the proposal, “Saying YES was the easiest decision I’ve ever made. I consider myself the absolute luckiest woman alive to live in your sphere. Aside from my father, without a doubt, you are the greatest man in the world. Emmy & Timmy said it’s about time mommy & daddy got engaged!!!”

Condor added of the ring, “💍: Anthony worked with the INCREDIBLE Vietnamese female owned @parisjewellerscanada to design the most stunning piece I’ve ever seen! The fact he knew how important it was to me that he’d collaborate w/ a Vietnamese company to help design my ring is a testament to just how truly thoughtful he always is. I cannot wait to be your wife, baby. I love you a million times over.”

The couple got married in Malibu at the Serra Franciscan Retreat Center, according to Vogue. Condor told the publication that she and her late mother Mary went to a spiritual retreat there when she was in high school.

“My mother Mary recently passed away, leaving my entire family devastated,” the actress shared. “But she would not have wanted us to not celebrate our love. We really wanted to get married at the Serra Retreat because being there was such a core memory that I had with my mom, and I felt like we would be able to feel her presence on our wedding night.”

The bride wore a strapless Vera Wang dress she picked out with her mother as well.

“My mom and I absolutely fell in love with my wedding gown the moment I tried it on, and I am so grateful I got to share that experience with her,” she said. “That was the only time she was able to see me in my dress, and I will hold that memory in my heart forever.”

She added, “The entire weekend could not have gone better. We are so filled with love and gratitude for each other and also for all of our incredible guests who came from far and wide to join us in the celebrations. This weekend will forever go down as the happiest weekend of our lives.”

The couple kicked off the year at Vanity Fair and Amazon MGM Studios’ awards season celebration in Los Angeles on January 10.

Condor wore a black Sebastian Gunawan Signature off-the-shoulder gown, while De La Torre opted for a copper-colored suit.

On March 15, they attended the Elton John AIDS Foundation’s annual Academy Awards viewing party in West Hollywood. Condor chose an ivory Alberta Ferretti halter-neck gown, while De La Torre wore a light blue suit.

They stepped out again on July 28 for a Los Angeles screening of The Devil’s Mouth, with Condor wearing a red gown featuring a plunging, draped neckline and De La Torre dressed in a black suit.

The pair also attended the US Open in New York City on September 8. Condor wore a navy top layered over a plaid collared shirt, accessorized with pink-tinted sunglasses, while De La Torre wore a blue blazer over a checked shirt.

In a Nov. 10, 2026 interview with People, Condor and De La Torre revealed that they spent their second wedding anniversary filming The Receipt, an upcoming romantic comedy that lets them share a love story on-screen, too.

“It was so cute. We got to celebrate it on set together with Fetch,” Condor said. “I don’t think we really told anyone. I think they just kind of knew and they were so, so sweet and everyone on set was just the most adorable. It was the cutest love-filled day. It was delightful.”

While they’ve worked together on music before, playing a couple on-screen was a new experience for Condor.

“I’ve fallen in love on camera quite a few times, but I think it’s a lot different when you can actually do it with someone that you share your life with and that you’ve loved for a very long time,” she said.

In addition to being an up-and-coming actor, De La Torre is also a musician. He’s previously been in bands including DE LA TORRE and The Fell. In October 2019, he released a new song titled “Know Me.” According to Seventeen, the track was written about Condor.

De La Torre and Condor teased the release of a joint musical project on Jan. 7, 2020. She shared a photo of the couple snuggled near a laptop on Instagram and captioned it, “We’ve been working on a very special project for months now for you guys. It’s been blood, sweat, and tears. It’s been highs & lows & sobs & laughs but we’ve done it together, and that’s what counts 💪🏽. Building dreams together. Love your loved ones, love your strangers. We can’t wait to share with you so soon x.”

Speculation that the couple was working on music together grew after De La Torre’s cryptic Instagram post. “Feb 19,” he captioned a shot of the pair.

The answer to questions about Condor and De La Torre’s first collaboration arrived with the dreamy duet, “Raining in London.” Condor announced the release of their song (and De La Torre’s EP) on Instagram. “This is our heart,” she wrote. “Our song 'Raining In London' (ft. me) is FINALLY OUT, and so is my dear @anthonydltorre whole EP FIND ME. I couldn’t be more proud of him. Against so many odds, he’s made this EP come to life for you all to hear. It’s definitely playing on repeat all day for me 💕! I love you golden boy, thru and thru.”

De La Torre also has his own channel, which includes a video of fun facts about the actor. In it, he opens up about learning to speak Spanish, his close bond with his siblings, and why he once dyed his hair black.

In the months since their first duet, the couple recorded a cover of Lauv’s “I Like Me Better” and released another original song, “No Way.” They even choreographed a TikTok dance for the latter song.

As for Condor’s musical catalog, she has also drawn inspiration from De La Torre. Last August, she released the song “For Real,” a track about how much she misses her longtime boyfriend while traveling for work.

“I’ve been in a pretty serious relationship for five years now, and whenever I travel I have to leave him,” she explained to People. “I always have a lot of anxiety about going off and leaving my home. I had just written down this journal entry about my feelings of going back to work as things start to shoot again, and my anxieties about leaving my boyfriend and about starting life up again, especially in this insane year that we've been having.”

Condor went on to address her new musical collaborations with De La Torre: “I mean, it seems so random, but I’ve always loved music. My boyfriend is a musician, and I’ve watched him navigate the music industry for years now, and I have always been very fascinated with it. I just felt like during quarantine, why not try something new if you love it?”

Condor told Cosmopolitan that when To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before was released in 2018, rumors about her and Centineo’s offscreen relationship persisted. “Noah and I definitely encouraged the speculation,” she admitted. “But it’s because we believe in the story and the characters, and we genuinely love each other. You can truly love someone in a very platonic way.”

But Condor said Instagram trolls would say “horrible things” about her real-life romance with De La Torre. “It was so hurtful for both of us,” she explained. “This is supposed to be a good thing. Why are you ­coming for someone who has no part of this story? If you say you support me, why would you hurt someone I love?”

For a while, De La Torre disabled his Instagram comments. But now, the couple seems to be handling social media pressure in stride. In August 2019, De La Torre even jokingly shared a photo with himself and Centineo, followed by a pic of Condor and co-star Madelaine Arthur. He captioned the post, “PK and LJ are even cuter in person.”

When De La Torre isn’t writing Condor love letters or sonnets, he’s providing another public service. Namely, he’s sharing photos with his and Condor’s dog Emmy. Yes, she has an Instagram:

Each couple experienced isolation a little differently during the COVID-19 pandemic. For Condor and De La Torre, they tried out TikTok trends and wore matching tie-dye outfits. Last March, both of the performers shared videos of themselves experimenting on the app to Instagram.

#6794. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6792. Time to Break Down That Major Twist at the End of ‘Campus Drivers’ and How It Differs From the Books

The Prime Video series takes a major turn from C.S. Quill’s series.

If—after watching the sixth episode of Campus Drivers—you wondered whether there were still more episodes to come, I’ve got bad news: That really was the finale. Season 1 of the French series from Prime Video ends with a twist that leaves plenty of questions, especially about Lane and Lois’s future. It’s a choice that even surprised those familiar with the book series by C.S. Quill, because the TV ending takes a different direction from the novel’s.

But what exactly happens in the final episode? And, above all, why does Lane decide to leave for Los Angeles right when his relationship with Lois finally seemed destined to take off? Let’s get into it.

In the season 1 finale, we discover that Lane leaves with his mother to move to L.A. and attend film school—a decision that, at first glance, seems to come out of nowhere, especially considering the bond that has formed with Lois over the course of the episodes. Behind this choice, however, there is a secret motivation: Lane’s mother does not approve of the path her son wants to take and believes his career choice is not the right one, but she accepts it on the condition that he leave Bellerose and end his relationship with Lois; otherwise, she is prepared to interfere with Lois’s scholarship, since she considers her an unsuitable partner for Lane.

At that point, Lane is faced with an impossible decision: stay beside the person he is falling in love with, or sacrifice his own happiness to allow her to continue studying medicine at a prestigious university.

Lane chooses the second option. He agrees to leave with his mother, thereby giving up the healthiest relationship of his life just when the two of them were finally beginning to truly grow closer.

The season’s final scene makes the separation even more concrete. In fact, we see movers carrying the furniture out of Lane’s apartment: an image that confirms his departure and the fact that the move to the United States is not just a threat or a possibility, but a decision that has now been made.

What’s more, Lois does not know the real reason for Lane’s departure. She does not know that he agreed to leave only to protect her future and allow her to continue her studies. In her eyes, that separation therefore risks seeming like a personal choice, a renunciation of their story just when it could have begun. At this point, the series departs considerably from the novel: the TV ending changes the protagonists’ journey and chooses to interrupt their story at a particularly delicate point, leaving the most important issue unresolved: the two protagonists did not get their romantic happy ending.

The ending seems designed precisely to leave the door open to a possible sequel (with the two of them still as major protagonists) and to heighten the drama—although for now, an official renewal has yet to be announced. Here’s hoping we will see Lane and Lois again in a second season!

#6790. John Legend, Lynn Nottage, and Whitney White on Reclaiming an American Classic From a Black Perspective

The three artists have teamed up on a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life

In 2020, against the dual backdrops of the isolating Covid-19 lockdown and the righteous fury of Black Lives Matter protests erupting across the country, John Legend and Lynn Nottage began exchanging messages. Though the EGOT-achieving musician and the two-time Pulitzer Prize–winning playwright couldn’t meet in person, the pair quietly became collaborators on a long-gestating project, now finally reaching the stage: a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life.

For nearly a century, Imitation of Life—which began as a 1933 best-selling novel by Fannie Hurst, became a John M. Stahl melodrama the following year, then was adapted for the screen again by Douglas Sirk in 1959—has been a cinematic fixture in Black American households, though not an uncontroversial one. Stahl’s iconic telling centers on Bea Pullman, a white widow and single mother, who takes in Delilah, a Black single mother, as a maid in exchange for housing her and her light-skinned daughter, Peola (Sarah Jane in Sirk’s version). When Bea boxes Delilah’s family pancake recipe and puts a caricature of her likeness on the package—an unmistakable nod to Aunt Jemima—she becomes a wealthy businesswoman. But Delilah, content simply to care for Bea and her daughter, turns down any share of the money. Meanwhile, Peola, seduced by her adjacency to whiteness, wrenchingly disavows her mother to pursue a life of passing.

As Sterling Brown’s scathing and influential 1935 review for Opportunity: A Journal of Negro Life made plain, the novel and film are mired in racist tropes. Yet despite its flaws, the story has continued capturing Black audiences, perhaps for the complex humanity clearly latent within Delilah’s and Peola’s characters. Now the story is being retold in a limited-run production opening November 21 at the Shed in New York, this time with a fully realized Delilah as the lead and an all-Black chief creative team at its core. Nottage wrote the book, while Legend—who also recently released his 11th studio album, Muse—composed the entire score. Whitney White, of Jaja’s African Hair Braiding and Liberation, directs, with Adrienne Warren starring as Delilah and Jessica Vosk as Bea. Leading up to rehearsals for the show, Nottage, Legend, and White—so in demand that she was pulled into a production meeting midconversation—took time to discuss their own relationships to Imitation of Life, their experience working together, and why the story’s themes remain as relevant as ever.

Lynn Nottage: I come from a generation where both films, Imitation of Life from the 1930s and 1950s, were fairly ubiquitous on television. I ended up seeing the films probably once or twice a year and found myself in sort of a tense, love-hate relationship with Imitation of Life, in that I was frustrated by the treatment of Delilah and Peola in 1934, then once again by Delilah and Sarah Jane in the 1950s. And yet I was fascinated by the racial politics and this young woman who had a really angsty relationship with her own identity. So I felt, when I was invited to work on this, that I had an opportunity to delve deeper into the character of Delilah and right some wrongs. She is so fascinating; she’s a mother, she’s an entrepreneur, she’s a woman who defies the expectations of the time. And yet throughout history, she has not been given her just deserts.

John Legend: I didn’t grow up knowing this film, but my parents and their generation know it very well. So when I told them that I was involved in this musical, they were so excited. And it made me realize how important this work was in our culture. But like Lynn, it was a fun challenge to reimagine the storytelling, where Delilah was centered, her emotionality was centered, her humanity was centered, and Peola’s humanity was centered as well.

And then, of course, I’ve never written a musical before. So part of the joy and educational experience that I’ve gotten from doing this is working with people who have so much brilliance and experience and understanding of a medium that I didn’t bring to the table. It was the perfect introduction, for me, to writing a musical. It’s been such a welcoming and edifying way to enter into this new branch of my career.

Nottage: John is a natural in this medium. It’s almost as if he had gotten a master’s degree in musical-theater writing. I think that, John, you have so many musicals in you and the world is going to be blown away.

Legend: Thank you. It really did make me think, why hadn’t I done this before?

Whitney White: For me, I was raised by a single mom, and she raised me at great personal cost—spiritual cost—in Chicago, on her own. So the 1930s film, I oddly was more familiar with it because I liked it more. I would sit with my grandmother when she was having her coffee, like, at whatever-o’clock in the morning, because neither of us would ever sleep. And for some reason, I attached myself to the 1930s film. The visuals of that film are so iconic. It’s like the foundation of Americana as it exists in global culture is visualized in that film. But also the story of Delilah echoes in so many ways. It’s almost like touching a stove that’s too hot for me. Because I’ve watched, my whole life, the world not see my mother in this way: her brilliance, her intelligence, her spunk, her grit. So I just knew that I could bring something to it. But I also knew that John and Lynn were going to give that character the eulogy and the eternal life that she’s always deserved.

Legend: Yeah, I think because of Lynn’s perspective and storytelling, we see other sides of Delilah. We see her being romantic. We see her being a brilliant businessperson, making a way out of no way—something that a lot of our ancestors, especially, had to do. They did what they had to do to realize the American dream.

Nottage: At the core, this is a story about the American dream, with all of its contradictions and complications. It’s a story about motherhood; it’s a story about friendship, but it’s also a story about passing. It’s what we are forced to do to negotiate and survive in a world that is sexist, in a world that is racist. Why, in America, do we constantly have to make compromises in order to reach for the things that we desire? Those sacrifices, most often, people of color have to make, women have to make. We are often asked to reshape our identities to fit into some sort of template that’s not necessarily comfortable for the shapes and forms of our bodies and our ideas and our identities. I think that’s why we keep coming back to this story, because the struggle remains real.

White: And I think, in the midst of having to constantly reshape our identities and bodies as Black folk, at the same time, we got the goods. We make culture. Here you have this character who saves this business, and what does she make? Delicious food that is worth millions of dollars. So while we’re forced to be at the bottom of the totem pole, we also make the goods that make capitalism work. That’s something I’ve really been sitting with: We’re so often plundered and taken from the most, and yet we are the foundation of capitalism.

Nottage: Can I ask you, Whitney, you’re looking particularly luminous today. Where are you going?

White: Girl, I’m sweaty. I just walked my child [to school]. My child is very heavy. I have this giant toddler; maybe he’s going to be LeBron. He didn’t want to walk to school, so he made me push him in his stroller.

Legend: I met your husband last week, so I see where he got it from.

Nottage: I totally digress, but I was like, “Look at you, you have, like, a rosy green glow.” [Laughs]

White: [Laughs] That beat that just happened is so beautiful because it’s so indicative of the show. Like, we’re talking about this heavy stuff, and then we all have this beautiful laugh. When I read the text and hear the music, it’s like I go through 10 cycles of emotions. I laugh, I cry, I fall in love, then I laugh and cry and fall in love again.

Legend: One of my favorite things from the workshops is watching the music come to life. You have all these amazing performers embodying all the things that I wrote in my little piano room at home.

Nottage: Theater is often so difficult and fraught to make because it is collaborative. I think one of the things that’s been really lovely about this process is the ease with which we, as collaborators, are capable of having conversations, because there’s so much mutual respect and appreciation. The bottom line in making a musical is that trust is necessary. It’s a tremendous leap of faith.

White [as she is pulled into production meeting]: I just want to add one last thing. Oh God, I don’t want to get too emotional. Never in my wildest dreams did I imagine that I would be sitting here with two of my favorite living artists. When I started in this business, this kind of makeup for a musical team was impossible. I can’t tell you how much pride and excitement that brings me, to be working alongside John and Lynn.

Nottage: Thank you. We love you, Whitney.

White: I’m going to go lovingly yell at people—lovingly, lovingly. Bye.

Legend: Our writing process started—we were meeting during Covid, so a lot of our first interactions were on Zoom. Lynn would send me pages with story dialogue and possible lyrical ideas. I would sit in my piano room and sketch out an idea with maybe a couple lyrics, but mostly just mumbling musical ideas around the theme.

Legend: One of the things that happens more in musical theater than I’m used to is the revision process and the workshopping process. … I’ve definitely done more revision in this show than I’ve ever done in my pop-music career.

Nottage: John, do you remember one of the very first conversations that we had? I said, “We’re probably going to write that opening-number song at least 15 times.” You’re like, “Oh, I don’t think so.” Here we are!

Legend: Here we are. We’ve rewritten it multiple times. [Laughs] I don’t have an extensive knowledge bank of musicals that I’ve seen and been a part of. So it was interesting, thinking about how to write for one, because part of it was like, “Well, let me just bring my own musical experience in the church and popular music and, you know, singing in a jazz a cappella group and singing show tunes in the choir in high school.” I just brought my own experience and said, “Let me just write the songs that I can be proud of and that I believe will work.” And then Lynn really taught me how to incorporate the storytelling into the songs.

Nottage: It’s so interesting to hear you say that, John, because I think one of the things that made the process so smooth is that you are a natural-born storyteller. There’s something that you understand instinctively and fundamentally about dramaturgy that I didn’t have to explain to you. I would venture to say that perhaps musicals are the hardest form of theater-making just because it is so layered.

But I also want to circle back to something that you said earlier, John, about the beginning of the process. It was Covid, and we were all thinking and making in isolation. There was something really lovely about reaching across the country to John and having these conversations about a book, even though we didn’t really, at that point, know each other. We spent a lot of time talking about ideas. It was absolutely thrilling. When I got that first mumble, I was like, “Oh my God, it’s John Legend mumbling, and it still sounds beautiful.” It was a song that we never, ultimately, ended up using. I think that eventually you should do an album of the outtakes.

Nottage: I just want to add, because we’ve been talking about some heavy themes, that the musical is not delivering spinach. The musical embodies all of the complexities of a beautiful woman like Delilah, a beautiful woman like Bea, a beautiful woman like Peola. It really is, in some ways, a celebration. It is a love letter. And it is a gift to be able to be in dialogue with these women.

#6787. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6783. The ‘Campus Drivers’ Finale May Have Revealed Who Season 2 Will Be About, and It’s a Change From the Books

Lois and Lane’s unfinished story could signal a major shift.

The season 1 finale of Campus Drivers left viewers with several questions, one of them being: Who will the protagonists of the next season be? Prime Video’s French-language series is based on the book series by C.S. Quill, but it has already demonstrated that it doesn’t necessarily intend to follow the novels’ path to the letter. And the very way Lane and Lois’s story ends could foreshadow an important narrative choice for the future of the adaptation.

As a reminder: In the series finale, Lane decides to leave for Los Angeles with his mother in order to allow Lois to keep her scholarship. Their story is cut short just when it seemed destined to turn into something more, leaving several questions open that could become central in a second season.

However, season 1 does also end with a twist involving Donovan. Without giving too much away about what happens in the second book, Crash Course, which is centered on Donovan, the ending sees (spoiler!) Donovan’s dad collapse during a basketball practice. Still, Lois and Lane remain at the center of the narrative with a story still entirely unresolved…and this choice may not be accidental.

The choice to keep Lane and Lois at the center of the story could suggest that Campus Drivers will not necessarily follow the narrative model of Off Campus. In the series based on Elle Kennedy’s novels, each season is designed to mainly tell the story of a new couple, giving space to different protagonists within the same narrative universe.

For Campus Drivers (despite the series having the same structure), the direction could be different: keeping Lane and Lois as central figures in the coming seasons as well, gradually developing the love stories of the other characters and giving them more and more space. Based on the season 1 ending, it’s possible that it will instead use an increasingly ensemble structure, in which the protagonists’ stories intertwine and become richer episode after episode, without necessarily making way for a new main couple each season.

This does not rule out the possibility that, further down the line, other characters could take on an even more important role. As early as the third season, for example, there could be room to explore the secondary love stories in greater depth. For now, however, these are only hypotheses; Prime Video has not yet confirmed what the structure of any future chapters will be.

If the series is renewed, it is reasonable to expect Lane and Lois to maintain a central role. The first season finale left their relationship hanging, and it would be difficult to imagine a follow-up that does not pick up precisely from what happened between them—especially because six episodes felt like too few to fully develop a dynamic between the leads.

The choice not to follow the structure of the novels could also be a way to differentiate Campus Drivers from Off Campus, especially since both series share the same platform and target the same audience of fans.

At the moment, a second season has not yet been officially confirmed. However, considering the attention the series is receiving and the narrative potential left open by the finale, it would not be surprising to see new updates arrive soon.

#6774. So...Beekeeper Matt Reportedly Got Engaged on ‘Love Is Blind,’ but the Show Left It Out

Here’s what we know about his fiancée—and where they currently stand.

I don’t know about you, but I’m personally exhausted by Love Is Blind leaving out a bunch of engagements from the show’s final edit. You’d think I’d have accepted it by now since it happens every season, but that just isn’t the case!

That being said, there’s another unseen engagement to come from the show. It turns out Matt Waldrip—aka Beekeeper Matt—fully proposed to someone in the pods, and it wasn’t Michaella Giorgio.

According to some new intel from Reality Receipts podcaster Rikkii Wise, Matt allegedly formed a meaningful connection with hairstylist and fellow single parent Jenn Mouser.

“We didn't see much of her on this season, but the two of them did go up to engagement day,” Rikki shared in her video.

Per Jenn’s official bio on Netflix’s Tudum, she joined the experiment because she felt “defeated by the dating apps.” She apparently had a goal of finding “a guy who’s also in touch with his softer side,” sharing, “Someone who’s not able to emotionally regulate gives me the absolute ick.” (Same, girl. Same.)

“I don’t know anything about the reveal or what happened after, but I know that these two didn’t date for long,” Rikki added.

Michaella also shared insight into Matt’s relationship status when she hopped on The Viall Files mic on November 11 and admitted she went on some dates with Matt “a few weeks after filming.”

As for Jenn, she seems to be recovering from a recent accident. According to a post shared on September 27, she said, “This past month has been the hardest of my life.”

“When I’m ready to post about the accident, I will. Please know I’m healing and feeling stronger everyday,” she wrote, in part. “Your love, support, prayers and donations have filled my heart with so much gratitude. I’m truly speechless.”

She separately shared a GoFundMe link to help raise donations for her recovery.

It’s unclear where Beekeeper Matt currently stands with Jenn, since there’s no evidence of their engagement on her social media profiles.

#6773. Gigi Hadid’s Guest In Residence Is Taking Its Signature Knits to the Slopes With Halfdays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like peanut butter and jelly or macaroni and cheese, cozy knits and skiwear just go together. After all, bundling up in layers is of the utmost priority when you’re hitting the slopes—and even more so when you’re taking part in some après-ski. So it only made sense that the female-founded and cool girl-approved ski brand Halfdays would team up with one of the coolest girls of our generation: Gigi Hadid—and more specifically, Hadid’s luxury knitwear brand, Guest In Residence.

“Most of my favorite winter memories are tied to being outside, getting dressed for the cold, and staying out until the last run,” Hadid shares in a press release. “With Halfdays, we wanted to bring that feeling into the Guest In Residence world, pairing our signature cozy warmth with technical pieces you actually want to wear on the mountain. The collection mixes sporty, vintage, nostalgic and playful influences, and is absolutely at home on the slopes.”

The retro-inspired collection—which is available to shop now—consists of eight pieces, including a corduroy ski bib, a coverall, a puffer jacket inspired by cable-knit sweaters, a Fair Isle turtleneck, and waffle-knit base layers. Combine with a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s alpine scene, and you have what is sure to be a statement on the slopes—and off. “As we think about how Halfdays can live beyond the slopes, knitwear felt like a natural next step,” Ariana Ferwerda, Halfdays’s co-founder and CEO, shared. “Guest In Residence brought its expertise in warmth and knitwear, while we brought our technical approach to mountain dressing. Together, we were able to create something that feels fashion-forward without compromising the performance women need on the mountain.”

Whether you’re the type who’s on the slopes until the ski lift closes or you prefer to stay at the lodge sipping on a hot toddy (or maybe you’re the girl who does both), this collection will keep you looking cute no matter how you prefer to spend your days on the mountain.
